It is important to note that there is no a priori optimal frequency of external monitoring in ECEC settings. 
Countries can choose to monitor certain dimensions of quality less often on average across the system 
while focusing efforts on specific settings (e.g. risk-based monitoring). However, results suggests that 
certain aspects of quality are not externally monitored in sizable percentages of settings. For instance, in 
six countries and subnational entities, 25% or more of pre-primary settings leaders report never being 
inspected on structural features (e.g. child-adult ratio, group size, staff qualification levels), and the same 
holds for the monitoring of staff practices (e.g. quality of interaction with children, content of activities); 
Morocco, Spain and Sweden are included in both groups. Similarly, more than 25% of pre-primary setting 
leaders in six countries and of leaders of settings for children under age 3 in another five countries and 
subnational entities report a lack of external inspections or monitoring of their setting’s financial records, 
including Germany and Israel at both levels (Table Leader.34). This suggests that countries are setting 
different priorities in their monitoring policies, and that inspections focused on process quality can, in some 
cases, occur in the absence of structural quality or financial inspections. 
In some countries, external monitoring is more frequent in private than in public settings 
Monitoring is also an important tool to ensure equity goals and steer public investment, for instance by 
enabling comparisons of quality and inclusion indicators between different types of settings. At the 
pre-primary level, privately managed settings are more frequently subject to external monitoring practices 
than publicly managed ones in several participating countries and subnational entities. This is most visible 
regarding inspections of financial records, which private pre-primary settings receive with greater regularity 
in 5 out of 11 countries and subnational entities where this comparison can be made (Table D.9.7). In 
addition, in three of these same countries, various aspects of quality are also more frequently monitored 
in privately managed settings: in Türkiye regarding requirements on facilities, structural quality features 
(e.g. child-adult ratios) and staff interactions with children; in Spain regarding facilities and structural 
quality; and in Finland regarding structural quality. Meanwhile, minimal differences exist in the frequency 
of external monitoring between public and private settings for children under age 3 (Table D.9.7). These 
results indicate that, at least at the pre-primary level, more intense external monitoring exists for privately 
than for publicly managed settings in several countries, particularly with regard to their finances, and 
presumably in line with accountability demands for public subsidies and quality standards. 
Internal monitoring of staff practices by setting leaders is widespread but follow-up 
feedback exchanges are less prevalent 
In 11 out of 15 countries and subnational entities at the pre-primary level and 7 out of 8 of those 
participating with settings for children under age 3, more than 50% of setting leaders report having 
observed staff practices and staff-child interactions weekly or more often over the 12 months prior to the 
survey. In Israel, this reaches more than 80% of leaders at both levels of ECEC, as is the case in Ireland, 
Japan and New Zealand** at the pre-primary level and in New Brunswick (Canada) in settings for children 
under age 3 (Table 9.2). While this shows that internal monitoring is a common practice in many ECEC 
systems, the percentage of settings where leaders report no or less than monthly observations of staff 
interactions with children remains above 30% in Colombia, Norway and Sweden at the pre-primary level, 
and at both levels of ECEC in the Flemish Community of Belgium (Table Leader.32).      
Sharing feedback with staff is, generally, less prevalent than observations of professional practices. In 6 
out of 15 countries and subnational jurisdictions at the pre-primary level and 5 out of 8 for settings for 
children under age 3, more than 50% of setting leaders report providing feedback to staff based on their 
observations with a weekly or higher frequency over the 12 months prior to the survey; in Israel for settings 
for children under age 3 this applies to more than two-thirds of settings. Meanwhile, in Morocco, Norway, 
Spain and Sweden in pre-primary settings, as well as in the Flemish Community of Belgium at both levels 

   219 
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
of ECEC, more than 30% of leaders report more infrequent (i.e. less than a monthly basis) or no feedback 
exchanges with staff based on observations of their practice (Table Leader.32).   
Overall, results point to a relatively underdeveloped culture of internal quality monitoring in ECEC in many 
of the participating countries and subnational entities, with leaders’ observations of staff’s professional 
activities being widespread but insufficiently matched by follow-up feedback exchanges, as both elements 
are necessary to spark improvement processes in setting-level practices. Internal monitoring practices also 
need to be considered in light of setting leaders’ responsibilities for guiding staff’s professional 
development and the assessment and monitoring of children’s outcomes. The provision of effective 
feedback to staff is indeed one of the areas where setting leaders tend to report a strong need for additional 
professional development (Table Leader.11).  
Leaders make changes to ECEC setting policies and practices based on monitoring 
results in several countries 
The percentage of setting leaders who report frequent changes (i.e. at least monthly over the 12 months 
prior to the survey) to policies and practices in their ECEC settings based on the results of monitoring and 
external evaluation varies notably across countries. This applies to around 20% of pre-primary settings in 
Denmark, Germany and Japan and around 10% of settings for children under age 3 in the Flemish 
Community of Belgium, compared to around 70% of settings in Israel and New Zealand** at both levels of 
ECEC (Table D.9.8). 
In some countries and subnational entities, pre-primary setting leaders are more likely to report 
adjustments in policies and practices when their settings receive external inspections in some areas at 
least once a year. This applies most consistently to Spain, where the frequency of inspections, be it of 
facilities, structural quality features, staff practices or financial records, is in all cases associated with a 
higher likelihood of leaders reporting changes in their settings. More frequent inspections of facilities are 
related to leaders adapting policies and practices in Germany, Morocco and Sweden, while more frequent 
financial audits are related to changes in Ireland and Morocco and the external monitoring of staff practices 
is associated with changes in Türkiye (Table D.9.8). Further, a higher frequency of leaders observing staff 
practices and staff-child interactions is also associated with a higher likelihood of changes to policies and 
practices in pre-primary settings in Colombia, Finland and Morocco. However, no significant differences 
are observed in the likelihood of changes based on the frequency of any of the areas of external or internal 
monitoring in settings for children under age 3 (Table D.9.8).  
Overall, the direction of the differences is, in all cases, consistent with the notion that settings’ policies and 
practices are more often adjusted based on the results of monitoring and evaluation activities when such 
activities, whether conducted by external inspectors or by setting leaders internally, take place more 
frequently, albeit this association is not statistically significant across all participating countries and 
subnational entities.  
Preparing for digital innovation  
A growing number of countries have introduced policies to ensure safe and meaningful uses of digital 
technologies in ECEC, with goals that include developing children’s early digital literacy, enhancing 
professional development opportunities for ECEC staff or promoting engagement with families, with 
protecting children from digital risks centre stage (OECD, 2023[15]; Eurydice, 2025[16]). An OECD policy 
survey conducted in 2022 shows that preparing ECEC staff to use digital technologies safely and effectively 
in their pedagogical work with children, particularly at the pre-primary level, was a high priority for most of 
the participating countries and subnational entities, but also that digital content is unevenly integrated into 
initial education and in-service training programmes for ECEC teachers (OECD, 2023[15]). ECEC 

220    
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
professionals are increasingly confronted with complex questions on whether and how to incorporate digital 
technologies into their work, especially when interacting with children, and at the same time asked to 
develop their own digital competencies to implement guiding frameworks (Wilson et al., 2023[17]; Zabatiero 
et al., 2018[18]). However, the implications of introducing digital devices into ECEC and educational settings 
at large remain an open debate, as evidence exists of both positive experiences and unintended drawbacks 
(OECD, 2025[19]). 
TALIS Starting Strong 2024 provides information on how ECEC staff and setting leaders engage with 
digital technologies for various purposes. This chapter focuses on staff preparedness (i.e. initial training, 
ongoing professional development, perceived need for further professional development and self-efficacy) 
to use technology specifically to facilitate working with children, as well as on the adequacy of settings’ 
digital infrastructure and staff’s views on the need for additional investment. Chapters 6 and 7 discuss the 
use of digital tools for professional development and management purposes. Chapter 7 also explores other 
dimensions of innovation in ECEC settings.  
A majority of staff have received training on the use of digital technologies to facilitate 
working with children 
According to staff, initial education or training programmes that included content on the use of digital 
resources and tools to facilitate working with children are particularly common in Chile, Colombia and 
Türkiye at the pre-primary level and in New Brunswick (Canada) in settings for children under age 3. In 
other countries and subnational entities, 40-70% of staff report this kind of training (in pre-primary settings 
and in settings for children under age 3), except in Japan, where just 25% of staff report having covered 
uses of digital technology as part of their initial preparation programme (Table D.9.9). To some extent, 
these patterns may reflect the recentness of initial training in the ECEC workforce: Colombia, 
New Brunswick (Canada) and Türkiye have relatively high shares of novice staff (with three years of 
experience or less), whereas staff in Japan are among the most experienced (Table D.2.10). 
In the majority of participating countries and subnational entities, and at both levels of ECEC, a smaller 
proportion of staff report that using digital solutions to facilitate working with children was included in their 
professional development activities in the 12 months prior to the survey compared to their initial training. 
Nonetheless, this percentage is over 50% in eight countries and subnational entities across the two levels 
of ECEC (Table D.9.9). 
Combining information on staff’s initial preparation programmes and recent training sheds light on the 
extent to which digital preparedness has become a target for professional development policies in ECEC 
internationally (Figure 9.4). In 10 out of 15 systems at the pre-primary level and in 5 out of 8 systems in 
settings for children under age 3, at least 65% of staff have received training on the use of digital tools to 
facilitate working with children, either in their initial education, ongoing professional development in the 
12 months prior to the survey or both (Table D.9.9). By contrast, in Denmark, Germany, Ireland, Japan 
and New Zealand** at the pre-primary level and in the Flemish Community (Belgium), Germany and Israel 
in settings for children under age 3, 35% or more of staff report that neither their initial preparation 
programmes nor their recent professional development activities included such content.

Staff who covered the use of digital tools with children in both their initial education and 
recent professional development have a higher sense of self-efficacy in this area  
ECEC staff’s self-efficacy for using digital technologies to facilitate working with children is generally the 
area of work about which staff express the least confidence among the areas captured by TALIS Starting 
Strong 2024, although there are great differences across the participating countries and subnational 
entities. At the pre-primary level, more than 70% of staff in Morocco, Spain and Türkiye feel they can use 
these technologies to facilitate working with children “quite a bit” or “a lot” while in Finland, Germany, Japan 
and Norway this is the case for less than 40% of staff, which mirrors differences across countries in other 
areas of perceived self-efficacy (Table Staff.24). Confidence for using digital technologies tends to be lower 
among staff working in settings for children under age 3, with between a third and half of staff reporting 
strong self-efficacy in this area in all countries and subnational entities except New Brunswick (Canada) 
and Quebec (Canada), where the percentages are around two-thirds of staff (Table D.9.10).  
Staff reports about further professional development needs for incorporating digital tools into their work 
with children provide a complementary perspective. At the pre-primary level, in two-thirds of the 
participating countries and subnational entities, more than 50% of staff report a “moderate” or “high” level 
of need in this area. In contrast, this level of need is only reported by staff working in settings for children 
under age 3 in Norway, where staff typically work in settings with pre-primary aged children as well as

as well as between staff at different levels of ECEC. Indeed, in the case of Norway, the curriculum

emphasises digital practices (Norwegian Directorate for Education and Training, 2017[20]). 
Across participating countries and subnational entities, there is a strong positive system-level association 
between the percentage of staff who report a high level of confidence in their ability to use digital tools to 
facilitate working with children and the percentage of staff having covered training content in this area in 
both their initial and recent ongoing training, both at the pre-primary level and in settings for children under 
age 3 (Figure 9.5). This suggests that countries and subnational entities where digitalisation is more 
consistently addressed in professional development programmes tend to have an ECEC workforce that 
feels better prepared to integrate digital tools into their work in ECEC settings.

more regular exposure to these topics throughout staff professional trajectories, rather than from recent 
training experiences or the imprint of initial education solely. Given the pace of change in digitalisation and 
young children’s exposure to new technologies, ongoing training at regular intervals is likely particularly 
important for building on foundational knowledge in this area. 
Few leaders perceive their setting’s digital infrastructure as a barrier to providing a 
quality environment for children 
At the pre-primary level, less than one in six setting leaders in Finland, the Flemish Community of Belgium, 
Japan, New Zealand** and Sweden report that shortages or inadequacies of digital resources hinder their 
setting’s capacity to provide a quality environment for children’s development, well-being and learning. 
This concern is somewhat higher in other countries and only shared among most leaders in Colombia and 
Morocco (Table D.9.13). Leaders’ reports about insufficient connectivity being a hindrance to a quality 
environment closely mirror those on the availability of other digital resources and tools, suggesting the 
need to address both types of inadequacies in concert. In most of the countries and subnational entities 
participating at the level of settings for children under age 3, smaller percentages of leaders report 
challenges due to poor digital infrastructure, which may reflect lower expectations on the need to use digital 
tools and the Internet to provide quality environments for children under age 3 (Table Leader.42). 
Within countries and subnational entities, differences in the reported adequacy of digital resources and 
connectivity tend to favour private over public settings in the countries where such gaps exist, such as 
Chile, the Flemish Community of Belgium, Morocco, Norway and Türkiye at the pre-primary level, or 
Germany at both levels of ECEC (Table D.9.13). Little differences are observed in the adequacy of digital 
infrastructure depending on the setting’s location, although more leaders of settings in small towns or rural 
areas than in cities report having poor infrastructure in Colombia and Morocco (Table D.9.14). Similarly, 
the size of the setting appears to be unrelated to the adequacy of digital technology in most participating 
countries and subnational entities, with the exception of Morocco (Table D.9.15). 
When asked about the priority of investing in digital resources if the budget for the ECEC sector as a whole 
were to increase by 5%, more than half of pre-primary staff in the majority of participating countries and 
subnational entities consider such investments to be of “somewhat high” or “very high” importance, 
whereas in Denmark, Finland, Germany, New Zealand**, Norway and Sweden, around a third or less of 
staff in pre-primary settings share this view (Table D.9.16). Across countries, and both at the pre-primary 
level and in settings for children under age 3, staff perceptions about the importance of investments in 
digital equipment appear to be moderately correlated with their reported professional needs or sense of 
self-efficacy: staff more in favour of such investments tend to also report a higher sense of self-efficacy in 
this area, potentially reflecting more positive attitudes about extending the use of digital tools in ECEC 
settings among staff who feel better prepared to use such tools with children. Staff who give greater 
importance to investments in digital resources also tend to express a greater need for additional 
professional development in this area, highlighting that availability of or resources for this type of ongoing 
training may be a perceived barrier to participation for many staff.  
Reducing stress from unexpected disruptions 
Situations of sudden and significant upheaval, such as natural disasters or public health emergencies, put 
the adaptative capacity of ECEC systems to the test. Ensuring the continuity and quality of ECEC provision 
in adverse circumstances requires retaining and supporting ECEC professionals so they can cope with 
disruptions and continue their work with young children and families (OECD, 2025[21]). Educational settings, 
including at the ECEC level, can play an important role in supporting children’s emotional recovery post-
disaster (Le Brocque et al., 2016[22]) but adapting to these situations can be highly stressful for ECEC staff 

224    
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
as well (Aydos et al., 2025[23]). This became visible during the COVID-19 pandemic, which introduced or 
magnified a range of stressors for the ECEC workforce, with negative effects on their mental health and 
well-being (Atiles et al., 2021[24]; Rodriguez et al., 2022[25]). Protecting ECEC professionals in the event of 
sudden crises is a concern, as increases in levels of stress and feelings of burnout tend to be associated 
with plans to leave the sector (Reinke et al., 2025[26]) (see Chapter 8).  
TALIS Starting Strong 2024 provides information on the work climate and well-being of ECEC staff and 
setting leaders. This section looks at staff and leader reports on the extent to which having to adapt their 
practices to unexpected disruptions (e.g. natural disasters, public health emergencies/pandemics, 
humanitarian crises) is a source of stress in their work, and how this varies across different types of ECEC 
settings (see Chapter 8 for a broader discussion on staff well-being and other sources of stress).   
Adapting to unexpected disruptions is more often a source of stress for setting leaders 
than for staff 
Unsurprisingly given their rarer occurrence, unexpected disruptions are less often reported as a source of 
stress by staff and leaders than most other potential stressors covered in the survey (Tables Staff.47 and 
Leader.45; see also Chapter 8). Nonetheless, at least 20% of staff in 7 out of 15 participating countries 
and subnational entities at the pre-primary level and in 3 out of 8 at the level of settings for children under 
age 3 report that adapting to unexpected disruptions represents “quite a bit” or “a lot” a source of stress in 
their work. This holds also for setting leaders in a higher number of systems at both levels of ECEC.  
The proportion of staff and setting leaders reporting disruption-induced stress is positively correlated 
across participating countries and subnational entities, which may reflect the relative frequency of these 
events and ECEC professionals’ levels of preparedness, but also country-specific patterns of response 
regarding stress factors (see Chapter 8). In a majority of cases, more setting leaders than staff tend to 
report these disruptions as highly stressful situations. This is most visibly the case in the Flemish 
Community of Belgium, Ireland, Japan and Norway at the pre-primary level, and in Norway and Quebec 
(Canada) in settings for children under age 3 (Table D.9.17). A more pronounced impact of disruptions on 
the stress levels of setting leaders, compared to staff, may reflect the particular dilemmas that leaders face 
during a crisis, when choices have to be made between competing demands regarding the organisation of 
ECEC settings (Rørstad Welle and Gunnulfsen, 2025[27]). Nonetheless, in other countries such as 
Colombia, Denmark or Türkiye, more staff than leaders report high levels of stress related to unexpected 
disruptions, perhaps related to staff autonomy or aspects of distributed leadership in these systems (see 
Chapter 7). 
Staff with more positive views on their setting leadership are less likely to experience 
stress from unexpected disruptions while staff more engaged in collaboration are more 
likely to find disruptions stressful  
Among the factors associated with staff experiencing stress from adapting their practice to unexpected 
disruptions are their overall satisfaction with their setting leadership and their engagement in collaborative 
practices within their settings (Figure 9.6) However, these associations run in opposite directions. Staff 
who hold more positive views about their setting leaders are less likely to report higher levels of stress 
from these situations when working in pre-primary settings in Chile, Finland, the Flemish Community of 
Belgium, Spain and Sweden, although the opposite is true in settings for children under age 3 in the 
Flemish Community of Belgium. By contrast, staff more frequently engaged in collaborative practices within 
their settings are more likely to cite disruptions as highly stressful in Chile, Denmark, Israel and Spain at 
the pre-primary level and in Ireland in settings for children under age 3. This may reflect stress related to 
disruptions in usual collaborative practices during times of crisis, or that staff anticipate having a greater 

   225 
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
role to play in addressing disruptions due to the collaborative nature of their work; in the latter case, 
collaboration may feel more like a job demand in times of crisis than a resource (see Chapter 8). 
Further, in some cases, other individual-level characteristics appear to be associated with this form of 
stress among staff. For instance, staff who identify as a teacher or leader, relative to other categories of 
staff, are more likely to report disruptions as a substantial source of stress in Japan at the pre-primary 
level, and in Germany, Ireland and Quebec (Canada) in settings for children under age 3 (Table D.9.18).  
Overall, results suggest that positive work relationships with setting leaders can be a major buffer to staff’s 
levels of stress in the event of unexpected crises, but also that staff who engage in peer collaboration more 
regularly and to some extent tend to have greater responsibilities within settings are more likely to confront 
stressful decisions when severe disruptions occur. This is in line with findings about overall levels of stress 
and workload increasing with staff’s work responsibilities and about setting leadership acting as a 
protective resource (see Chapter 8).

Several OECD countries are adopting strategies for ECEC services to respond to crises, including through 
the development of setting-level action plans and training and practical resources to improve staff 
preparedness and resilience to stress (Box 9.1).  
Box 9.1. Coping with disruptions in early childhood education and care: Policies and practices 
to support settings and staff  
Ireland: Responding to critical incidents in ECEC services 
In Ireland, the Quality and Regulatory Framework of the Tusla Child and Family Agency, responsible 
for the Early Years Inspectorate, requires all early childhood education and care (ECEC) settings to 
have a level plan to respond to critical incidents. These include events such as severe weather, public 
health emergencies, significant damage to facilities or criminal attacks, but also incidents during outings 
or the death of a child enrolled in the service. In 2023, a national working group developed a practical 
guide to assist ECEC settings in establishing those plans (Critical Incident National Working Group with 
Barnardos, 2024[28]). 
Guidance emphasises reducing the effects of stress and minimising the risk of further problems as key 
rationales for early and systematic responses to critical incidents. Setting-level plans outline actions to 
be taken at a time of heightened feelings and anxiety; these actions should be communicated to and 
understood by staff before crises occur. Critical incident plans cover four key stages: 1) prevention 
(identifying and managing risks); 2) preparing (identifying appropriate actions); 3) responding 
(implementing immediate, secondary and follow-up actions); and 4) reviewing (reflecting on the 
experience as a team and with families and revising the plan). The guide also includes checklists, 
sample plans, communication templates and other resources to help settings plan for each stage. 
City and county childcare committees run training aligned with this guidance and provide support to 
implement suggested actions. In turn, the Department of Children, Disability and Equality holds 
responsibility for co-ordinating a national response to critical incidents in ECEC. This involves an 
exchange of information between local and national levels; local support for ECEC settings and staff; 
force majeure payments for service closure; and sustainability funding for longer support, including 
extra staffing and therapeutic interventions. 
New Zealand: Disaster preparedness in ECEC 
In New Zealand, all ECEC services are required to have an emergency management plan in place. 
These plans must be regularly reviewed and include procedures for emergencies, evacuations, 
contacting emergency services, and providing medical treatment and assistance (New Zealand Ministry 
of Education, 2024[29]). Given New Zealand’s diverse natural hazard profile, including earthquakes, 
floods, landslides, storms, tsunamis and volcanic activity, ECEC services tailor their plans to local risks. 
To support rapid communication during emergencies, the Ministry of Education uses a contact tool 
called Mataara, which enables swift outreach to ECEC settings. Additionally, the National Emergency 
Management Agency provides resources specifically designed for ECEC settings, including guidance 
on preparing children for emergencies and advice on how to talk to children about natural hazards. One 
widely adopted practice is Turtle Safe, a child-friendly version of the “Drop, Cover, and Hold” earthquake 
drill, which teaches children to respond like a turtle retreating into its shell (Get Ready, n.d.[30]). 
Regional emergency management offices also hold community trainings for ECEC staff. These 
sessions use short videos, interactive activities and presentations to cover a range of topics, including 
parent-child reunification procedures and business continuity planning. Emergency preparedness in 

   227 
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
Policy pointers 
• 
Strengthen engagement with families and co-operation with other sectors. Social and 
institutional connections help ECEC settings to attend to children’s needs within their family and 
community contexts and in synergy with other services, but gaps in communication with families 
between public and private settings and limited engagement with other sectors and primary 
schools limit this capacity in many countries. Policies can support ECEC settings’ local 
connections by providing resources for supporting family engagement, particularly in publicly 
managed settings, and by fostering co-operation with other early years services, independent of 
the setting’s proximity to other institutions. Chile and Colombia, for collaboration with health-related 
services and home-visits; the Flemish Community of Belgium and Spain, for co-operation with 
primary schools; and Denmark, Germany and New Zealand, for co-operation across settings and 
with other support services for young children, illustrate strategies to strengthen institutional 
connections in ECEC settings. 
• 
Promote collaborative work practices for all staff within ECEC settings. Job-embedded peer 
collaboration is a major avenue for effective professional learning and a consistent predictor of 
staff’s adoption of child-centred and adaptive pedagogical approaches. Policies can support 
collaborative practices within ECEC settings to provide protected staff time for group work and 
feedback exchanges, leadership that supports pedagogical learning, staff agency and 
collaboration among staff, as well as by ensuring that all staff roles (not only teachers) have 
opportunities to collaborate meaningfully. Staff in New Zealand and Norway strongly engage in 
multiple collaborative activities. In Chile, Germany, Morocco and Sweden at the pre-primary level, 
and Ireland and Israel in settings for children under age 3, leadership practices promote staff 
collaboration. 
• 
Expand the scope and, where needed, the frequency of quality monitoring activities in 
support of improvement processes. Effective monitoring can stimulate quality improvement in 
ECEC and help ensure equitable access to quality services. Across countries, however, external 
inspections more often focus on structural aspects than on process quality, while frequent internal 
monitoring of staff-child interactions is not always followed by guidance to staff. Monitoring policies 
can give greater attention to process quality alongside structural aspects and focus on quality 
improvement over accountability, with concrete support for setting leaders, such as making 
feedback exchanges with staff an integral component of internal monitoring activities. Morocco 
and Spain are examples of countries where policies and practices in pre-primary settings are more 
often adjusted based on the results of quality monitoring activities, according to setting leaders. 
• 
Provide regular training opportunities on the use of digital technology in ECEC settings. 
While countries need not necessarily expand digital infrastructure in ECEC settings, the rapid pace 
of digitalisation makes it critical to ensure staff has up-to-date knowledge on the potential risks and 
benefits of digital tools for young children and can take informed decisions about integrating them 
(or not) into their work. Staff’s sense of self-efficacy for using digital tools in interactions with 
children is higher when they have covered this area in both their initial education and ongoing 
professional development activities, which points to training policies that address digitalisation with 
ECEC is supported through alignment with New Zealand’s National Disaster Resilience Strategy and 
co-ordination with regional civil defence emergency management groups. The ministry’s guidance on 
managing traumatic incidents highlights the importance of structured support for staff well-being, 
recommending measures such as buddy systems, relief staffing, regular debriefs, and ongoing 
monitoring to reduce stress and prevent compassion fatigue.  

228    
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
a consistent approach. Colombia, Morocco, Spain and Türkiye at the pre-primary level, and 
New Brunswick (Canada) in settings for children under age 3, are systems where higher shares 
of staff have received training in this area both during their initial preparation and recently.   
• 
Invest in crisis preparedness, including in measures to protect the well-being of ECEC 
professionals. Adapting work practices to unexpected disruptions is a substantial source of stress 
for ECEC staff and setting leaders, with potential negative effects on retention. With the incidence 
of extreme climate and other events predicted to increase in the near future, policies can aim to 
improve the preparedness of ECEC settings and staff to respond to crises through training and 
resources that clarify procedures and responsibilities in adverse circumstances. Ireland and 
New Zealand provide examples of national strategies to anticipate and improve response to 
disaster events in ECEC, including requirements for detailed setting-level action plans, training for 
staff and measures to support the continuity of services (Box 9.1).

Annex A. Profiles of early childhood education 
and care systems 
The profiles in this annex provide an overview of early childhood education and care (ECEC) systems 
across participating countries and subnational entities, to contextualise findings from the Starting Strong 
Teaching and Learning International Survey (TALIS Starting Strong) 2024. These profiles present system-
level data that highlight key policies and structures shaping ECEC provision. The profiles draw primarily 
from OECD policy questionnaires and indicators, such as those published in Education at a Glance and 
the Starting Strong series. In some cases, supplementary information was provided by participating 
countries and subnational entities and is noted in the sources at the end of this annex. 
Each profile is structured around two core elements: 
1. A figure illustrating the organisation of the ECEC system, including legal entitlements to ECEC, the 
types and age groupings of ECEC settings, the presence of a curriculum or learning framework, 
and governance arrangements. 
2. A table presenting statistical indicators and policy information across six key dimensions: 
• 
enrolment in ECEC 
• 
working conditions 
• 
expenditure and funding 
• 
governance 
• 
quality standards 
• 
workforce development.

3.5. Fee regulations and 
targeted support 
Public ECEC services are provided free of charge. For private ECEC services, there is no national 
framework regulating parental fees in ECEC; subsidised centres receive per-child funding that varies by 
region, with additional support available for children with special educational needs and those from 
disadvantaged backgrounds. 
4. 
Governance 
4.1. Responsibility for setting 
a regulatory framework 
The Ministry of Education (Ministerio de Educación) is responsible for setting a regulatory framework for 
ECEC settings. 
4.2. Responsibility for 
monitoring settings 
The implementation of the Curricular Bases for Early Childhood Education (Bases Curriculares de la 
Educación Parvularia) is monitored by the central government, while the implementation of the 
Framework for Good Teaching at Early Childhood Education (Marco para la Buena Enseñanza de la 
Educación Parvularia) involves co-ordination across central, regional and local government levels. 
5. 
Quality standards 
5.1. Group size and child-
staff ratios 
For all setting types: 
• 
For children under age 2: one assistant per 7 children; one teacher per 42 children and one 
food operator per 40 children. Maximum group size is 21. 
• 
For children age 2: one assistant per 25 children and one teacher per 32 children. Maximum 
group size is 32. 
• 
For children age 3: one assistant and one teacher per 32 children. Maximum group size is 32. 
• 
For children age 4: one assistant and one teacher per 35 children. If the group size is ten or 
less, only one teacher is required. According to current regulations, there is no maximum 
group size. 
• 
For children age 5-6: one assistant and one teacher per 45 children. If the group size is 15 or 
less, only one teacher is required. According to current regulations, there is no maximum 
group size. 

234    
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
Indicator 
Description 
5.2. Coverage of curriculum 
frameworks 
The implementation of the Curricular Bases for Early Childhood Education (Bases Curriculares de la 
Educación Parvularia) is mandatory for all ECEC settings, while the implementation of the Framework for 
Good Teaching at Early Childhood Education (Marco para la Buena Enseñanza de la Educación 
Parvularia) is recommended for all settings. 
6.Workforce development 
6.1. Minimum initial 
educational attainment 
required for ECEC 
classroom/playroom 
teachers2 
For all ECEC settings, classroom/playroom teachers are required to have an ISCED level 6 education 
(bachelor’s degree). 
6.2. Yearly participation in 
professional development for 
staff4 
For publicly managed and funded kindergartens and for teachers and assistants that work in schools, 
minimum yearly participation in professional development is required and regulated by the government. 
1. Enrolment rates include the number of children enrolled in ISCED 01 and ISCED 02 programmes. 
2. Teachers are the individuals with the most responsibility for a group of children at the classroom or playroom level and may be referred to as 
pedagogues, educators or childcare practitioners. They have varying levels of qualification across countries but are generally expected to hold 
qualifications commensurate with the professional nature of their work, often a tertiary degree. 
3. Data include education-related expenditure by households spent outside institutions (e.g. books, pencils).  
4. Staff refers to individuals whose professional activity involves the care and transmission of knowledge, attitudes and skills to children enrolled 
in an ECEC setting. This definition does not depend on the qualification held by the ECEC staff or on the delivery mechanism. ECEC staff may 
include teachers, educators, assistants or staff working with individual children, among other categories. 
Notes: ECEC: early childhood education and care; ISCED: International Standard Classification of Education; PPP: purchasing power parity; 
GDP: gross domestic product.

3.5. Fee regulations and targeted

settings

Monitoring System, administered by the Ministry of National Education (Ministerio de Educación 
Nacional) in co-ordination with the Intersectoral Commission for Comprehensive Early Childhood 
Care (Comisión Intersectorial para la Atención Integral de la Primera Infancia).

ratios 
• 
First cycle of initial education  
Child-staff ratios: 
o 
For children aged 3-12 months: 10:1 
o 
For children aged 12-23 months: 10:1 
o 
For children aged 24-36 months: 15:1 
o 
For multi-age groups (6-36 months): 10:1 
o 
If more than 4 children under 23 months are enrolled in multi-age groups, one 
pedagogical assistant is required. 
• 
Second cycle of initial education 
Child-staff ratios in maximum numbers: 
o 
For pre-kindergarten, kindergarten and multigrade second cycle, the ratios are 
20:1 in urban settings and 15:1 in rural settings 
o 
For transition grades, the ratios vary from 20:1 to 25:1 for urban settings and 
from 15:1 to 18:1 in rural settings. 
o 
For flexible strategies for early childhood education in dispersed rural areas 
ratios vary from 8:1 to 12:1. 
• 
For the ECEC services provided by the Colombian Institute of Family Welfare, the 
operational structure must comply with the technical manuals applicable to each modality.

Indicator 
Description 
5.2. Coverage of curriculum 
frameworks 
The Curriculum Bases for Initial and Preschool Education (Bases curriculares para la educación 
inicial y preescolar) is a guiding document for teachers in all ECEC settings. 
6. 
Workforce development 
6.1. Minimum initial educational 
attainment required for ECEC 
classroom/playroom teachers2 
For teachers working in the second cycle of initial education, a bachelor’s degree or equivalent 
corresponding to ISCED level 6 is required. Training programmes are recommended to include 
content-specific knowledge relevant to early childhood education, such as child development, 
curriculum and pedagogy, and special education. For other ECEC settings, classroom and playroom 
teachers are required to hold at least an ISCED level 4 qualification, typically a technical degree in 
early childhood care or education. 
6.2. Yearly participation in 
professional development for staff3 
All providers of ECEC services are encouraged to implement an annual professional development 
plan for staff. 
1. Enrolment rates include the number of children enrolled in ISCED 01 and ISCED 02 programmes. 
2. Teachers are the individuals with the most responsibility for a group of children at the classroom or playroom level and may be referred to as 
pedagogues, educators or childcare practitioners. They have varying levels of qualification across countries but are generally expected to hold 
qualifications commensurate with the professional nature of their work, often a tertiary degree. 
3. Staff refers to individuals whose professional activity involves the care and transmission of knowledge, attitudes and skills to children enrolled 
in an ECEC setting. This definition does not depend on the qualification held by the ECEC staff or on the delivery mechanism. ECEC staff may 
include teachers, educators, assistants or staff working with individual children, among other categories.

5. 
Quality standards 
5.1. Group size and child-staff ratios 
For kindergartens, the child-staff ratio is 6:1. 
5.2. Coverage of curriculum 
frameworks 
The Strengthened Pedagogical Curriculum (Den styrkede pædagogiske læreplan) is 
compulsory for all ECEC settings for all age groups. 
6. 
Workforce development 
6.1. Minimum initial educational 
attainment required for ECEC 
classroom/playroom teachers2 
To qualify as a classroom/playroom teacher in a kindergarten, nursery or an integrated 
institution, a bachelor’s degree with content-specific knowledge of ECEC is required, 
corresponding to ISCED level 6. 
6.2. Yearly participation in professional 
development for staff3 
There is no regulation mandating annual professional development. 
1. Enrolment rates for under age 3 include the number of children enrolled in ISCED 01 and rates for children age 3-5 includes ISCED 02 programmes and primary 
education, for purposes of international comparison. 
2. Teachers are the individuals with the most responsibility for a group of children at the classroom or playroom level and may be referred to as pedagogues, 
educators or childcare practitioners. They have varying levels of qualification across countries but are generally expected to hold qualifications commensurate 
with the professional nature of their work, often a tertiary degree. 
3. Staff refers to individuals whose professional activity involves the care and transmission of knowledge, attitudes and skills to children enrolled in an ECEC 
setting. This definition does not depend on the qualification held by the ECEC staff or on the delivery mechanism. ECEC staff may include teachers, educators, 
assistants or staff working with individual children, among other categories. 
Notes: ECEC: early childhood education and care; ISCED: International Standard Classification of Education; PPP: purchasing power parity; GDP: gross domestic 
product.

3.5. Fee regulations and targeted 
support 
Pre-primary education (esiopetus) (around four hours per day) is free of charge. Children have a 
legal right to access ECEC before and after pre-primary hours, with fees determined by family 
income, size and hours of attendance. 
4. 
Governance 
4.1. Responsibility for setting a 
regulatory framework 
Ministry of Education and Culture (Suomen opetus- ja kulttuuriministeriö) 
 
4.2. Responsibility for monitoring 
settings 
National authority 
5. 
Quality standards 
5.1. Group size and child-staff 
ratios 
Centre-based early childhood education (päiväkoti): 
• 
The child-staff ratio for children under age 3 is 4:1, meaning one qualified staff member 
per four children. The maximum group size is 12 children with 3 staff members. 
• 
For children age 3-5, the ratio is 7:1, with a maximum group size of 21 children per 3 staff 
members. A ratio of 13:1 may be applied if children attend for a maximum of 5 hours per 
day, with a maximum group size of 39 children; however, such groups do not exist in 
practice in Finland, despite being permitted by regulation. 
Family day care (perhepäivähoito): 
• 
Each childminder may care for up to four children, plus one part-time child (e.g. a child 
attending pre-primary education who joins for the afternoon). In group family day care, the 
same ratio applies, with a maximum of 3 staff members (at least one with higher 
qualifications) and 12 children in total. 
Open early childhood education and care activities (avoin varhaiskasvatustoiminta): 
• 
There are no regulated child-staff ratios. 
5.2. Coverage of curriculum 
frameworks 
The curricula is compulsory for all ECEC settings. There is no separate curriculum for open ECEC 
services and family day care; however, the National Core Curriculum for Early Childhood Education 
and Care (esiopetuksen opetussuunnitelman perusteet) is also applied in these settings, with 
necessary adaptations.

6. 
Workforce development 
6.1. Minimum initial educational 
attainment required for ECEC 
classroom/playroom teachers2 
There are no formal educational qualification requirements to become a classroom/playroom teacher 
in an open ECEC activities setting. In a home-based early childhood education setting, an upper 
secondary education with content-specific knowledge related to ECEC is required, corresponding to 
ISCED level 3. To work as a classroom/playroom teacher in either a centre-based early childhood 
education setting or a pre-primary education setting, a bachelor’s degree with content-specific 
knowledge related to ECEC is required, corresponding to ISCED level 6. 
6.2. Yearly participation in 
professional development for staff4 
Yearly participation in professional development for centre-based early childhood education and pre-
primary education is regulated. 
1. Enrolment rates include the number of children enrolled in ISCED 01 and ISCED 02 programmes. 
2. Teachers are the individuals with the most responsibility for a group of children at the classroom or playroom level and may be referred to as 
pedagogues, educators or childcare practitioners. They have varying levels of qualification across countries but are generally expected to hold 
qualifications commensurate with the professional nature of their work, often a tertiary degree. 
3. Data on pre-primary education include salaries of kindergarten teachers. 
4. Staff refers to individuals whose professional activity involves the care and transmission of knowledge, attitudes and skills to children enrolled 
in an ECEC setting. This definition does not depend on the qualification held by the ECEC staff or on the delivery mechanism. ECEC staff may 
include teachers, educators, assistants or staff working with individual children, among other categories.

3.5. Fee regulations and targeted 
support 
In subsidised childcare settings (kinderopvang), parent contributions are income-based. In contrast, 
settings that do not benefit from any public subsidies, or receive only a public basic funding, have 
discretion to set their own fees. Pre-primary education (kleuteronderwijs) is provided free of charge, 
with families only covering the cost of meals. 
4. 
Governance 
4.1. Responsibility for setting a 
regulatory framework 
• 
For childcare settings (kinderopvang), the responsibility is with the Flemish Minister of 
Welfare and Poverty Reduction, Culture and Equal Opportunities (Vlaams minister van 
Welzijn en Armoedebestrijding, Cultuur en Gelijke Kansen) 
• 
For pre-primary education settings (kleuteronderwijs), the responsibility is with the 
Inspectorate of Educational Quality, which belongs to the Policy domain of Education 
and Training. 
4.2. Responsibility for monitoring 
settings 
• 
For childcare settings (kinderopvang), the responsibility is with the Flemish Healthcare 
Inspectorate in close collaboration with the Agency Opgroeien. 
• 
For pre-primary education settings (kleuteronderwijs), the regional and sub-regional 
governments are in charge of monitoring. 
5. 
Quality standards 
5.1. Group size and child-staff 
ratios 
For childcare settings (kinderopvang), the child-staff ratios were updated in 2024 and are being 
implemented with a transition phase until 2027: 
• 
5:1 for groups with only children younger than 12 months. 
• 
8:1 in groups with only children older than 12 months. 
• 
7:1 in mixed groups. 
For pre-primary education settings (kleuteronderwijs), there is no specified child-staff ratio.

5.2. Coverage of curriculum 
frameworks 
• 
Childcare settings (kinderopvang) are recommended to implement the pedagogical 
framework introduced by the Flemish Minister of Welfare and Poverty Reduction, Culture 
and Equal Opportunities (Vlaams minister van Welzijn en Armoedebestrijding, Cultuur en 
Gelijke Kansen). 
• 
Pre-primary education settings (kleuteronderwijs) are required to develop an educational 
plan aligned with the developmental goals (ontwikkelingsdoelen) set by the Flemish 
Ministry of Education and Training (Vlaams Ministerie van Onderwijs en Vorming). These 
plans need to be approved by the government.

Workforce development 
6.1. Minimum initial educational 
attainment required for ECEC 
classroom/playroom teachers3 
• 
In childcare settings (kinderopvang), staff are required to hold a qualification at ISCED

workers. 
• 
In pre-primary education settings (kleuteronderwijs), a bachelor’s degree in pre-primary 
education at ISCED level 6 is required to work as a qualified pre-primary teacher. 
6.2. Yearly participation in 
professional development for staff4 
Both childcare (kinderopvang) and pre-primary education settings (kleuteronderwijs) are required to 
provide training in professional development, although there are no requirements on the content, 
intensity or delivery mode. 
1. Enrolment rates cover children enrolled in ECEC services, including ISCED and other registered programmes, for those under age 3, and 
children enrolled in pre-primary education (ISCED 02) for those age 3-5, for purposes of international comparison. Pre-primary education in the 
Flemish Community of Belgium also includes children age 6 and the other indicators in this profile reflect the situation for the full age-range of 
the pre-primary education system. 
2. Data reflect regional-level statistics reported for the Flemish Community of Belgium. 
3. Teachers are the individuals with the most responsibility for a group of children at the classroom or playroom level and may be referred to as 
pedagogues, educators or childcare practitioners. They have varying levels of qualification across countries but are generally expected to hold 
qualifications commensurate with the professional nature of their work, often a tertiary degree. 
4. Staff refers to individuals whose professional activity involves the care and transmission of knowledge, attitudes and skills to children enrolled 
in an ECEC setting. This definition does not depend on the qualification held by the ECEC staff or on the delivery mechanism. ECEC staff may 
include teachers, educators, childcare professionals, assistants or staff working with individual children, among other categories.

3.5. Fee regulations and targeted 
support 
Regulations on parental fees vary across federal states. In some states, such as Berlin and 
Mecklenburg-Western Pomerania, fees have been fully removed for both centre-based and family 
childcare. Other states waive fees for children age 3 and above (e.g. Brandenburg, Bremen, Lower 
Saxony) or offer fee-waivers for a specific amount/number of hours, either across all age groups (e.g. 
Hamburg) or specifically for children over age 3 (e.g. Hesse). In Saxony-Anhalt, parents are required 
to pay contributions only for their oldest child enrolled in ECEC. In addition, all federal states are 
required to apply socially staggered contributions for ECEC, typically based on household income, 
number of children or hours of attendance, although the specific rules differ across states. Low-
income families receiving child allowance or social benefits (e.g. housing or unemployment 
allowance, or asylum seekers benefits) are generally exempted from paying fees. 
4. 
Governance 
4.1. Responsibility for setting a 
regulatory framework 
Subnational authorities (Länder/federal states) are responsible for setting a regulatory framework. 
4.2. Responsibility for monitoring 
settings 
The responsibility for monitoring ECEC settings lies with the 16 federal states (Länder), each of 
which have differing approaches that can involve different levels of local/sub-regional and regional 
oversight. 
5. 
Quality standards 
5.1. Group size and child-staff 
ratios 
Regulations regarding group size and child-staff ratios are determined by each of the 16 federal 
states (Länder) and differ significantly across states. 
5.2. Coverage of curriculum 
frameworks 
The Common Framework for Early Education in ECEC settings (Gemeinsamer Rahmen der Länder 
für die frühe Bildung in Kindertageseinrichtungen) sets out common principles for pedagogical 
practice across the country. However, each federal state also maintains its own curriculum 
framework, which differ in their coverage of ECEC settings. 
6. 
Workforce development 
6.1. Minimum initial educational 
attainment required for ECEC 
classroom/playroom teachers2 
• 
For family childcare settings (kindertagespflege), a pedagogical qualification of any sort, 
typically the qualification course to work in family childcare, is required as a minimum 
(minimum 160-hour course, alternatively a 300-hour course or a higher qualification). 
• 
For all other ECEC setting types, a degree equivalent to a bachelor’s including content-
specific knowledge to ECEC is required, corresponding to an ISCED level 6. 

248    
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
Indicator 
Description 
6.2. Yearly participation in 
professional development for staff3 
• 
For family childcare (kindertagespflege), regulations are set at the state level. In most 
states, annual participation in professional development is required (e.g. Bavaria, North 
Rhine-Westphalia, Hesse, Saxony-Anhalt, Mecklenburg-Western Pomerania). 
• 
For ECEC settings for mixed age groups (kindertageseinrichtungen mit Kindern aller 
Altersgruppen), children under age 3 and children age 3-6, yearly participation in 
professional development for staff is required and regulated. 
1. Enrolment rates include the number of children enrolled in ISCED 01 and ISCED 02 programmes. Pre-primary education in Germany also 
includes children age 6 and the other indicators in this profile reflect the situation for the full age-range of the pre-primary education system. 
2. Teachers are the individuals with the most responsibility for a group of children at the classroom or playroom level and may be referred to as 
pedagogues, educators or childcare practitioners. They have varying levels of qualification across countries but are generally expected to hold 
qualifications commensurate with the professional nature of their work, often a tertiary degree. 
3. Staff refers to individuals whose professional activity involves the care and transmission of knowledge, attitudes and skills to children enrolled 
in an ECEC setting. This definition does not depend on the qualification held by the ECEC staff or on the delivery mechanism. ECEC staff may

Notes: ECEC: early childhood education and care; ISCED: International Standard Classification of Education; PPP: purchasing power parity;

3.5. Fee regulations and targeted 
support 
Universal and targeted subsidies, based on family income and other factors, are available for 
children from 6 months of age to support participation in ECEC. These subsidies cover hours beyond 
those provided by “Early Childhood Care and Education programme”, which provides 2 years of free 
preschool provision (15 hours per week during term-times). 
4. 
Governance 
4.1. Responsibility for setting a 
regulatory framework 
Department of Children, Disability and Equality 
4.2. Responsibility for monitoring 
settings 
National authority 
5. 
Quality standards 
5.1. Group size and child-staff 
ratios 
Child-staff ratios: 
• 
for 0-1-year olds: 3:1 
• 
for 1-2-year-olds: 5:1 
• 
for 2-3-year-olds: 6:1 
• 
for 3-5-year-olds: 8:1 for full-time and part-time care and 11:1 for sessional care (up to 3.5 
hours per day). 
Group size: 
ECEC settings that only provide a “sessional preschool service” (catering for 3-5-year-olds for up to 
3.5 hours per day) can have no more than 22 children in a room at a time. This requirement does not 
apply if the service involves full-time care. 
5.2. Coverage of curriculum 
frameworks 
The curriculum framework is recommended for use across all ECEC settings and age groups. 
6. 
Workforce development 
6.1. Minimum initial educational 
attainment required for ECEC 
classroom/playroom teachers2 
• 
To qualify as a classroom/playroom staff (including teachers) in centre-based ECEC 
settings, post-secondary non-tertiary education is required, corresponding to ISCED level 
4. 
• 
There is no qualification requirement for home-based ECEC settings, except for a 
requirement to complete a short pre-registration training course. 
6.2. Yearly participation in 
professional development for staff3 
Yearly participation in professional development for staff in centre-based settings is neither regulated 
nor required. 
1. Enrolment rates include the number of children enrolled in ISCED 01 programmes for those under age 3, and pre-primary (ISCED 02) and 
primary education (ISCED 1) for those aged 3-5, for purposes of international comparison. 
2. Teachers are the individuals with the most responsibility for a group of children at the classroom or playroom level and may be referred to as 
pedagogues, educators or childcare practitioners. They have varying levels of qualification across countries but are generally expected to hold 
qualifications commensurate with the professional nature of their work, often a tertiary degree. 
3. Staff refers to individuals whose professional activity involves the care and transmission of knowledge, attitudes and skills to children enrolled 
in an ECEC setting. This definition does not depend on the qualification held by the ECEC staff or on the delivery mechanism. ECEC staff may 
include teachers, educators, assistants or staff working with individual children, among other categories. 
Notes: ECEC: early childcare education and care; ISCED: International Standard Classification of Education; PPP: purchasing power parity; 
GDP: gross domestic product.

3.5. Fee regulations and targeted 
support 
For children age 0-3, access to subsidised ECEC is available for up to 50 hours per week 
(approximately 40% of all registered centre-based settings). If there are more children than there 
are places available, an admission committee will assess children’s needs against priority criteria. 
4. 
Governance 
4.1. Responsibility for setting a 
regulatory framework 
The Ministry of Education (Mis’rad Ha’Chinuch) is responsible for setting a regulatory framework 
for ECEC settings. 
4.2. Responsibility for monitoring 
settings 
The Ministry of Education’s (Mis’rad Ha’Chinuch) supervisors. 
5. 
Quality standards 
5.1. Group size and child-staff ratios 
6. 
For childcare centres (meonot), the child-staff ratio is 6:1 for children under the age of 15 
months; 9:1 for children under age 2; 9:1 for children age 2-3. 
7. 
For family childcare (mishpachtonim), the child-staff ratio is 5:1 in publicly managed settings 
and 6:1 in privately managed settings. 
8. 
For formal (publicly managed) and unofficial (privately managed) kindergartens (ganei-
yeldim), group size is limited to 35 children. Child-staff ratios are 30:1 for teachers and 1 
assistant for up to 30 children and 35:1 for teachers and 2 assistants for 30-35 children. 
5.2. Coverage of curriculum 
frameworks 
The guidelines for pre-primary education are compulsory for kindergartens. For settings for 
children under age 3, compliance with the developmental plan provided by the Ministry of 
Education (Mis’rad Ha’Chinuch) is one of the criteria assessed by supervisors. 
6. 
Workforce development 
6.1. Minimum initial educational 
attainment required for ECEC 
classroom/playroom teachers2 
• 
To qualify as a classroom/playroom teacher in a kindergarten (ganei-yeldim), a 
bachelor’s degree in early childhood education is required, corresponding to ISCED 
level 6. 
• 
In Ultra-Orthodox settings, teachers are required to complete a culturally adapted 
programme, corresponding to ISCED level 5. 
• 
To qualify as a classroom/playroom teacher or assistant in a nursery (only in childcare 
centres (Meonot)), completion of 220 hours of childcare training offered by the Ministry 
of Education (Mis’rad Ha’Chinuch) is required, corresponding to ISCED level 4. 
6.2. Yearly participation in 
professional development for staff3 
For all ECEC settings, minimum yearly participation in professional development is a common 
practice but not required by the government. 
1. Enrolment rates include the number of children enrolled in ISCED 01 programmes for those under age 3, and pre-primary (ISCED 02) and 
primary education (ISCED 1) for those age 3-5, for purposes of international comparison. 
2. Teachers are the individuals with the most responsibility for a group of children at the classroom or playroom level and may be referred to as 
pedagogues, educators or childcare practitioners. They have varying levels of qualification across countries but are generally expected to hold 
qualifications commensurate with the professional nature of their work, often a tertiary degree. 
3. Staff refers to individuals whose professional activity involves the care and transmission of knowledge, attitudes and skills to children enrolled 
in an ECEC setting. This definition does not depend on the qualification held by the ECEC staff or on the delivery mechanism. ECEC staff may 
include teachers, educators, assistants or staff working with individual children, among other categories. 
Notes: ECEC: early childhood education and care; ISCED: International Standard Classification of Education; PPP: purchasing power parity; 
GDP: gross domestic product.

3.5. Fee regulations and targeted 
support 
Universal entitlement to some ECEC free of tuition fees. 
Public kindergartens, daycare centres, centres for early childhood education and care, and 
municipal-level childcare services are provided free of charge for all children age 3-5 as well as for 
some children from low-income households age 0-2. Private settings are free up to the same 
amount as public settings. 
4. 
Governance 
4.1. Responsibility for setting a 
regulatory framework 
• 
The Children and Families Agency (Kodomokateichou) is responsible for daycare centres 
(hoikusho) and centres for early childhood education and care (ninteikodomoen). 
• 
The Ministry of Education, Culture Sports, Science and Technology (Monbukagakushou) 
is responsible for centres for early childhood education and care (ninteikodomoen) and 
kindergartens (youchien). 
4.2. Responsibility for monitoring 
settings 
In daycare centres (hoikusho) and centres for early childhood education and care (ninteikodomoen), 
monitoring is carried out both at the regional, sub-regional and setting levels. In kindergartens 
(youchien), monitoring is carried out by the national government, regional or sub-regional 
authorities, school corporations, and social welfare corporations, which are the entities that establish 
and approve them.

5. 
Quality standards 
5.1. Group size and child-staff 
ratios 
• 
Daycare centre (hoikusho) 
Group size: – 
Child-staff ratios: 
o 
For children under age 1: 3:1. 
o 
For 1- to 2-year-olds: 6:1. 
o 
For 3-year-olds: 15:1. 
o 
For 4- to 5-year-olds: 25:1. 
• 
Centres for early childhood education and care (ninteikodomoen) 
Group size: maximum 35 children for ages 3-5 
Child-staff ratios: 
o 
For children under age 1: 3:1. 
o 
For 1- to 2-year-olds: 6:1. 
o 
For 3-year-olds: 15:1. 
o 
For 4- to 5-year-olds: 25:1. 
o 
For children over age 3: 1 ECEC teacher per class. 
• 
Kindergartens (youchien) 
Group size: no more than 35 children. 
Child-staff ratios: 1 teacher per class. 
5.2. Coverage of curriculum 
frameworks 
The curriculum corresponding to each type of ECEC setting is mandatory across all setting types. 
6. 
Workforce development 
6.1. Minimum initial educational 
attainment required for ECEC 
classroom/playroom teachers2 
To qualify as a classroom/playroom teacher in any of the ECEC settings, a short-cycle tertiary 
degree with content-specific knowledge to ECEC is required, corresponding to ISCED level 5. 
6.2. Yearly participation in 
professional development for staff4 
In daycare centres (hoikusho), centres for early childhood education and care (ninteikodomoen), 
and kindergartens (youchien), yearly participation in professional development for staff is common 
practice and recommended. In the public sector, kindergarten teachers and ECEC teachers in 
centres for early childhood education and care are obligated to participate in statutory professional 
development such as induction training. 
1. Enrolment rates include the number of children enrolled in ISCED 01 and ISCED 02 programmes. 
2. Teachers are the individuals with the most responsibility for a group of children at the classroom or playroom level and may be referred to as 
pedagogues, educators or childcare practitioners. They have varying levels of qualification across countries but are generally expected to hold 
qualifications commensurate with the professional nature of their work, often a tertiary degree. 
3. Data do not cover daycare centres or centres for early childhood education and care. 
4. Staff refers to individuals whose professional activity involves the care and transmission of knowledge, attitudes and skills to children enrolled 
in an ECEC setting. This definition does not depend on the qualification held by the ECEC staff or on the delivery mechanism. ECEC staff may 
include teachers, educators, assistants or staff working with individual children, among other categories. 
Notes: ECEC: early childhood education and care; ISCED: International Standard Classification of Education; PPP: purchasing power parity; 
GDP: gross domestic product.

3.5. Fee regulations and targeted 
support 
Public preschool classes (age 4-5) (                                                ) 
are free of charge; private provision is fee-based. Targeted programmes (e.g. through partnerships) 
support expansion in disadvantaged areas. Private provision (age 0-3) is fee-based. 
4. 
Governance 
4.1. Responsibility for setting a 
regulatory framework 
Regulation is led by national authorities, with responsibilities split across types of provision: 
• 
Public-partnered preschool education (التعليم األولي العمومي المسير من طرف شركاء 
 القطاع) 
is under the supervision of the Ministry of National Education, Preschool and Sports, but 
delegated to associations and foundations. 
• 
Private preschool is authorised by the Ministry of National Education, Preschool and 
Sports. 
• 
Traditional Quranic schools (Kouttab, M’sid) 
        (                       (
 )      
are 
linked to the Ministry of Habous and Islamic Affairs. 
• 
Nurseries and social care services partly fall under the Entraide Nationale within the 
Ministry of Solidarity, Social Integration and Family and the Ministry of Youth, Culture 
and Communication. 
4.2. Responsibility for monitoring 
settings 
Monitoring is led by national authorities, with responsibilities split across types of provision: 
• 
Public-partnered preschool 
(
)التعليم األولي العمومي المسير من طرف شركاء القطاع 
classrooms and most private preschools are monitored by the Ministry of National 
Education, Preschool and Sports. 
•

(

Ministry of Habous and Islamic Affairs. 
• 
Nurseries/daycare

) are overseen in part by Entraide Nationale within the

supervision.

5. 
Quality standards 
5.1. Group size and child-staff 
ratios 
The average child-to-staff ratio is 18.8 (2023/2024); ratios vary by region and provider. 
5.2. Coverage of curriculum 
frameworks 
The National Curriculum Framework is being rolled out in public-partnered classrooms; most 
unregulated kindergartens and traditional Quranic schools (Kouttab, M’sid) remain outside its 
formal scope. The National Curriculum Framework is not mandatory for private preschool. 
6. 
Workforce development 
6.1. Minimum initial educational 
attainment required for ECEC 
classroom/playroom teachers2 
The minimum requirements to qualify as an ECEC classroom/playroom teacher to work in public-
partnered preschool classrooms are holding at least a high school diploma (ISCED level 3), being 
proficient in the children’s mother tongue, and in a foreign language (posséder a minima un 
baccalauréat [diplôme de fin du secondaire qualifiant], maîtriser la langue maternelle des enfants 
ainsi qu’une langue étrangère). 
6.2. Yearly participation in 
professional development for 
staff3 
Participation is not mandated nationally and varies by provider and region; the Moroccan 
Foundation for the Promotion of Preschool Education organises regular annual training for its 
educators. 
1. Enrolment rates include the number of children enrolled in ISCED 0 and other registered programmes, for purposes of international 
comparison. 
2. Teachers are the individuals with the most responsibility for a group of children at the classroom or playroom level and may be referred to as 
pedagogues, educators or childcare practitioners. They have varying levels of qualification across countries but are generally expected to hold 
qualifications commensurate with the professional nature of their work, often a tertiary degree. 
3. Staff refers to individuals whose professional activity involves the care and transmission of knowledge, attitudes and skills to children enrolled 
in an ECEC setting. This definition does not depend on the qualification held by the ECEC staff or on the delivery mechanism. ECEC staff may 
include teachers, educators, assistants or staff working with individual children, among other categories. 
Notes: ECEC: early childhood education and care; ISCED: International Standard Classification of Education; PPP: purchasing power parity; 
GDP: gross domestic product.

3.5. Fee regulations and targeted 
support 
Designated ECEC settings that receive operational grants are required to follow a Market Fee 
Threshold and Standard Parent Fee Grid, which cap parent contributions and limit fee increases. 
Disadvantaged families are also supported through income-tested subsidies (Parent Subsidy 
Program and Childcare Assistance Program), and targeted inclusion funding for children with 
disabilities. 
4. 
Governance 
4.1. Responsibility for setting a 
regulatory framework 
Department of Education and Early Childhood Development 
4.2. Responsibility for monitoring 
settings 
• 
Early learning and childcare facilities/garderies éducatives under the New Brunswick 
Curriculum Framework for Early Learning and Child Care or the New Brunswick 
Francophone Curriculum Framework for Early Learning and Chilcare (Curriculum éducatif 
Services de garde francophone du Nouveau-Brunswick) are monitored by regional or sub-
regional governments. 
• 
Francophone kindergarten programmes (maternelle) are monitored by the central 
government. 
• 
Anglophone kindergarten programmes are monitored by district education authorities. 
5. 
Quality standards 
5.1. Group size and child-staff 
ratios 
Licensed settings may enroll up to 60 children, with ratios and group sizes set by age: 
• 
Infants (under 24 months): 3:1, max. 6. 
• 
Two-year-olds: 5:1, group size: 10. 
• 
Three-year-olds: 8:1, group size: 16. 
• 
Four-year-olds: 10:1, group size: 20. 
• 
School-age (5-12 years): 15:1, group size: 30. 
• 
Mixed age groups: ratios are set for the group based on the ages of the children. 

   261 
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
Indicator 
Description 
5.2. Coverage of curriculum 
frameworks 
• 
The New Brunswick Curriculum Framework for Early Learning and Child Care and the 
New Brunswick Francophone Curriculum Framework for Early Learning and Chilcare 
(Curriculum éducatif Services de garde francophone du Nouveau-Brunswick) are required 
for use in designated early learning and childcare facilities/garderies éducatives. With the 
exception of school-age children, the framework is common to both anglophone and 
francophone sectors. In the francophone sector, a specific curriculum for school-age 
children exists (the Cadre d’orientation du Curriculum des garderies éducatives 
francophones du Nouveau-Brunswick pour les enfants d’âge scolaire). In the anglophone 
sector, a school-age curriculum is currently under development. 
• 
The New Brunswick Curriculum Framework for Kindergarten/Curriculum maternelle is part 
of a broader K-8 continuum of learning. Within this continuum, the Primary curriculum 
applies to Grades K-2, while the Kindergarten curriculum/Curriculum maternelle is specific 
to the kindergarten year and is compulsory for all kindergartens/maternelles. 
6. 
Workforce development 
6.1. Minimum initial educational 
attainment required for ECEC 
classroom/playroom teachers2 
Initial qualification requirements for staff differ by type of provision and age group served: 
• 
In centre-based ECEC, at least half of direct-care staff, including the director or designate, 
are required to hold a one-year certificate, diploma or equivalent with ECEC-specific 
content, corresponding to ISCED level 4. Staff without this qualification are required to 
complete the provincial Introduction to Early Childhood Education course, which includes 
30 hours of training in the New Brunswick Curriculum Framework. 
• 
In home-based ECEC settings, there are no formal requirements for minimum initial 
educational attainment, although ECEC-specific initial education attainment is 
encouraged. However, providers without ECEC-specific initial education are required to 
complete the 30-hour Curriculum Framework training and a 60-hour orientation when 
working with children age 0-5. 
• 
For staff exclusively working with school-age children, there are no formal requirements 
for ECEC-specific initial education. 
• 
Out-of-province teachers with recognised ECEC credentials are also required to complete 
the 30-hour Curriculum Framework training. 
6.2. Yearly participation in 
professional development for staff3 
• 
For early learning and childcare facilities/garderies éducatives, participation is neither 
regulated nor required. 
• 
For kindergartens/maternelles, participation is not mandatory, but is a common practice. 
• 
Staff in designated ECEC settings are required to complete at least ten hours of 
professional training annually. 
1. Enrolment rates include the number of children enrolled in ISCED 01 and other registered programmes, for purposes of international 
comparison. 
2. Teachers are the individuals with the most responsibility for a group of children at the classroom or playroom level and may be referred to as 
pedagogues, educators or childcare practitioners. They have varying levels of qualification across countries but are generally expected to hold 
qualifications commensurate with the professional nature of their work, often a tertiary degree. 
3. Staff refers to individuals whose professional activity involves the care and transmission of knowledge, attitudes and skills to children enrolled 
in an ECEC setting. This definition does not depend on the qualification held by the ECEC staff or on the delivery mechanism. ECEC staff may 
include teachers, educators, assistants or staff working with individual children, among other categories. 
Notes: ECEC: early childhood education and care; ISCED: International Standard Classification of Education; PPP: purchasing power parity; 
GDP: gross domestic product.

RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
Indicator 
Description 
5.2. Coverage of curriculum 
frameworks 
The curriculum corresponding to each type of ECEC setting is compulsory across all settings. 
6. 
Workforce development 
6.1. Minimum initial educational 
attainment required for ECEC 
classroom/playroom teachers2 
• 
There is no minimum qualification for staff in centre-based or hospital-based services; 
however, at least 50% of required staff must hold a recognised qualification, typically a 
degree-level qualification in early childhood education, corresponding to ISCED level 6. 
Services are incentivised through the funding system to employ a higher proportion of 
certificated teachers, with higher funding rates provided per child hour when more 
qualified staff are employed. 
• 
In home-based services, educators are required to hold at least a Level 4 home-based 
qualification or to be working towards it within six months of starting employment, 
equivalent to ISCED level 4. 
6.2. Yearly participation in 
professional development for staff4 
Yearly participation in professional development for staff is common practice but not regulated in 
kindergartens and home- and centre-based education and care settings. It is neither regulated nor 
required for play centres.
