pedagogues, educators or childcare practitioners. They have varying levels of qualification across countries but are generally expected to hold 
qualifications commensurate with the professional nature of their work, often a tertiary degree. 
3. Expenditure includes government sources excluding private and domestic spending.  
4. Staff refers to individuals whose professional activity involves the care and transmission of knowledge, attitudes and skills to children enrolled 
in an ECEC setting. This definition does not depend on the qualification held by the ECEC staff or on the delivery mechanism. ECEC staff may 
include teachers, educators, assistants or staff working with individual children, among other categories. 
Notes: ECEC: early childhood education and care; ISCED: International Standard Classification of Education; PPP: purchasing power parity;

Legal 
entitlement 
 
 
 
 
All children are legally entitled to a place in a publicly subsidised setting and family 
childcare from an early age (6-18 months) and municipalities are responsible for 
guaranteeing access to ECEC for all eligible children.

Child-staff ratio: 
• 
for children under age 3: 3:1. 
• 
for children age 3 and above: 6:1. 
In addition, the pedagogue norm sets requirements for teacher staffing. Kindergartens (barnehange) 
are required to employ at least one qualified teacher for every 7 children under age 3 and one 
qualified teacher for every 14 children age 3 and above. 
5.2. Coverage of curriculum 
frameworks 
Compulsory for all settings and groups. 
6. 
Workforce development 
6.1. Minimum initial educational 
attainment required for ECEC 
classroom/playroom teachers2 
ECEC classroom/playroom teachers are required to hold a preschool teacher qualification or another 
three-year pedagogical degree with additional training in early childhood education, corresponding to 
ISCED 6.

professional development for staff3 
Not mandatory, but participation each year is common practice.

Legal 
entitlement 
 
 
The Educational Childcare Act establishes a right for all children from birth until school entry to access 
educational childcare, subject to availability; attendance is not compulsory.

3.5. Fee regulations and targeted 
support 
• 
Subsidised ECEC settings, including both centre-based and home-based settings, charge 
a regulated daily fee, with reduced rates available for lower income families through 
additional financial assistance. 
• 
Non-subsidised settings, including both centre-based and home-based settings, set their 
own fees, but parents may receive refundable tax credits based on income. 
4. 
Governance 
4.1. Responsibility for setting a 
regulatory framework 
The government of Quebec is responsible for establishing the legal and regulatory framework related 
to the ECEC settings, according to the Educational Childcare Act (Section 106, Educational Childcare 
Act, Chapter S-4.1.1). 
4.2. Responsibility for monitoring 
settings 
• 
The ministry overseeing family-related matters (Ministère de la Famille du Québec) is 
directly responsible for monitoring and ensuring compliance with the legal and regulatory 
framework. 
• 
For home-based educational childcare services (garde éducative en milieu familial), the 
co-ordinating offices for home-based educational childcare, which are accredited by the 
ministry overseeing family-related matters, are responsible for ensuring compliance with 
the standards and conditions required for recognition. 
• 
The government of Quebec also works closely with certain Indigenous partner 
organisations, to whom it has delegated specific responsibilities for monitoring and 
enforcing the legal and regulatory framework for certain indigenous ECEC services. 

   269 
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
Indicator 
Description 
5. 
Quality standards 
5.1. Group size and child-staff 
ratios 
• 
For centre-based settings (centre de la petite enfance/garderie éducative): 100 children 
maximum per setting, based on the capacity of the space and distributed according to 
defined age groups. In addition, centre-based settings are required to respect the following 
child-staff ratios: 
o 
5:1 for children under 18 months. 
o 
8:1 for children 18 months to under 4 years. 
o 
10:1 for children aged 4-5 years. 
o 
20:1 for children aged 5 years and older. 
• 
For home-based settings (garde éducatif en milieu familial): 6 children per setting if a 
provider is working alone; 9 children per setting if the provider is assisted by another adult. 
5.2. Coverage of curriculum 
frameworks 
Each childcare service is required to implement its educational programme, which reflects its mission, 
values and vision. This programme outlines the guidelines for educational intervention and includes 
information to guide the educational staff. It is recommended that educational programmes be 
inspired by the reference framework Welcoming Early Childhood (Acceuillir la petite enfance). 
6. 
Workforce development 
6.1. Minimum initial educational 
attainment required for ECEC 
classroom/playroom teachers2 
• 
For centre-based settings (centre de la petite enfance/garderie éducative): A minimum of 
an ISCED level 5 diploma specialised in early childhood education or equivalent is 
required. 
• 
For home-based settings (garde éducatif en milieu familial): If the provider does not hold 
an ISCED level 5 diploma, they are required to complete at least 45 hours of training in 
health, safety, nutrition, child development and the educational programme. 
6.2. Yearly participation in 
professional development for staff5 
• 
For centre-based settings (centre de la petite enfance/garderie éducative): Participation in 
professional development is not regulated or required. 
• 
For home-based settings (garde éducatif en milieu familial): Participation in professional 
development is required at least for six hours annually to maintain setting status. 
1. Enrolment rates include the number of children enrolled in ISCED 01 programmes. 
2. Teachers are the individuals with the most responsibility for a group of children at the classroom or playroom level and may be referred to as 
pedagogues, educators or childcare practitioners. They have varying levels of qualification across countries but are generally expected to hold 
qualifications commensurate with the professional nature of their work, often a tertiary degree. 
3. Information was provided by Quebec in Canadian dollars (CAD) and was converted to US dollars (USD) using PPPs for private consumption 
reference year 2023 as provided by Education at a Glance 2025, see Annex A sources.  
4. Expenditure on ECEC was provided by Quebec as CAD 3.8 billion, plus CAD 0.2 billion in subsidies for infrastructure investments, for the 
period April 2023 to March 2024. To calculate expenditure as a share of GDP, the provincial GDP was averaged across 2023 (CAD 437 230 
million) and 2024 (CAD 442 865 million), resulting in CAD 440 048 million. The expenditure figure of CAD 4.0 billion was then divided by the 
average GDP. Source for GDP: Statistics Canada, Gross domestic product (GDP) at basic prices, by industry, province and territory. 
5. Staff refers to individuals whose professional activity involves the care and transmission of knowledge, attitudes and skills to children enrolled 
in an ECEC setting. This definition does not depend on the qualification held by the ECEC staff or on the delivery mechanism. ECEC staff may 
include teachers, educators, assistants or staff working with individual children, among other categories. 
Notes: ECEC: early childhood education and care; ISCED: International Standard Classification of Education; PPP: purchasing power parity; 
GDP: gross domestic product.

4. 
Governance 
4.1. Responsibility for setting a 
regulatory framework 
Ministry of Education (Ministerio de Educacion) 
4.2. Responsibility for monitoring 
settings 
The educational authorities of the autonomous communities are responsible for monitoring ECEC 
settings in public and government-funded private schools. 
5. 
Quality standards 
5.1. Group size and child-staff 
ratios 
• 
For early childhood education – first cycle (Educación infantil – Primer ciclo), the child-
staff ratio varies depending on the autonomous community and age group: 
o 
for children under 1 year old: the ratios vary from 6:1 to 8:1. 
o 
for 1-year-old children: the ratios vary from 10:1 to 14:1. 
o 
for 2-year-old children: the ratios vary from 16:1 to 20:1. 
• 
Early childhood education – second cycle (Educación infantil – Segundo ciclo), Article 7 of 
Royal Decree 132/2010 establishes a maximum of 25 pupils per class group and teacher. 
5.2. Coverage of curriculum 
frameworks 
The organisation and minimum curriculum requirements for ISCED 01 and ISCED 02 are regulated 
at the national level by Royal Decree 95/2022. Building on these national requirements, the 
educational authorities of the autonomous communities, through their respective regional ministries 
and departments of education, develop the ECEC curriculum for both levels. 
6. 
Workforce development 
6.1. Minimum initial educational 
attainment required for ECEC 
classroom/playroom teachers2 
To qualify as a classroom/playroom teacher in an early childhood care and education setting, a short-
cycle tertiary degree with content-specific knowledge of ECEC is required, corresponding to ISCED 
level 5. To qualify as a classroom/playroom teacher in a pre-primary setting, a bachelor’s degree with 
content-specific knowledge of ECEC is required, corresponding to ISCED level 6. 
6.2. Yearly participation in 
professional development for staff3 
– 
1. Enrolment rates include the number of children enrolled in ISCED 01 and ISCED 02 programmes. 
2. Teachers are the individuals with the most responsibility for a group of children at the classroom or playroom level and may be referred to as 
pedagogues, educators or childcare practitioners. They have varying levels of qualification across countries but are generally expected to hold 
qualifications commensurate with the professional nature of their work, often a tertiary degree. 
3. Staff refers to individuals whose professional activity involves the care and transmission of knowledge, attitudes and skills to children enrolled 
in an ECEC setting. This definition does not depend on the qualification held by the ECEC staff or on the delivery mechanism. ECEC staff may 
include teachers, educators, assistants or staff working with individual children, among other categories. 
Notes: ECEC: early childhood education and care; ISCED: International Standard Classification of Education; PPP: purchasing power parity; 
GDP: gross domestic product.

4.2. Responsibility for monitoring 
settings 
Municipalities are responsible for monitoring private ECEC settings in their municipality and the 
Swedish Schools Inspectorate (Skolinspektionen) is responsible for monitoring the settings on a 
municipality level. 
5. 
Quality standards 
5.1. Group size and child-staff 
ratios 
There is no regulation for the group size; however, the Swedish National Agency for Education 
recommends 6-12 children per group aged 1-3 years. For children aged 4-5 years, the guideline is 9-
15 children per group. There is no regulation or guideline for child-staff ratios. 
5.2. Coverage of curriculum 
frameworks  
The Curriculum for the Preschool (Läroplan för förskolan) applies to preschools (förskola) and 
preschool classes (förskoleklass). Pedagogical care (pedagogisk omsorg), regulated under “other 
pedagogical activities” in the Education Act (Skollagen), has no separate curriculum, but the preschool 
curriculum serves as guidance. 
6. 
Workforce development 
6.1. Minimum initial educational 
attainment required for ECEC 
classroom/playroom teachers2 
To qualify as a childminder for pedagogical care (pedagogisk omsorg), there are no formal educational 
qualification requirements. To become a classroom/playroom teacher in a preschool setting (förskola 
and förskoleklass), a bachelor’s degree with content-specific knowledge related to ECEC is required, 
corresponding to ISCED level 6. 
6.2. Yearly participation in 
professional development for staff4 
There is no regulation mandating annual professional development. The National Agency for Education 
(Skolverket) provides subsidies to municipalities to support staff training, but participation is voluntary. 
1. Enrolment rates include the number of children enrolled in ECEC services, including ISCED 0 and other registered programmes, for purposes 
of international comparison. Pre-primary education in Sweden also includes children aged 6 and the other indicators in this profile reflect the 
situation for the full age-range of the pre-primary education system. 
2. Teachers are the individuals with the most responsibility for a group of children at the classroom or playroom level and may be referred to as 
pedagogues, educators or childcare practitioners. They have varying levels of qualification across countries but are generally expected to hold 
qualifications commensurate with the professional nature of their work, often a tertiary degree. 
3. Data exclude social security contributions and pension scheme contributions paid by employees. 
4. Staff refers to individuals whose professional activity involves the care and transmission of knowledge, attitudes and skills to children enrolled 
in an ECEC setting. This definition does not depend on the qualification held by the ECEC staff or on the delivery mechanism. ECEC staff may 
include teachers, educators, assistants or staff working with individual children, among other categories.  
Notes: ECEC: early childhood education and care; ISCED: International Standard Classification of Education; PPP: purchasing power parity; 
GDP: gross domestic product.

3.5. Fee regulations and 
targeted support 
ECEC is free for children over age three. Families may pay a contribution fee for basic needs and programme 
implementation, except low-income families who are exempt. Targeted supports include conditional cash 
transfers to encourage enrolment, stationary assistance for disadvantaged children and free textbooks for all 
children. 
4. 
Governance 
4.1. Responsibility for 
setting a regulatory 
framework  
The Ministry of National Education (Milli Eğitim Bakanlığı) is responsible for setting a regulatory framework for 
ECEC settings. 
The Ministry of Family and Social Services (Aile ve Sosyal Hizmetler Bakanlığı) is responsible for the regulation 
and licensing of childcare centres (özel kreş ve gündüz bakımevleri). 
4.2. Responsibility for 
monitoring settings 
– 
5. 
Quality standards 
5.1. Group size and 
child-staff ratios 
• 
For pre-primary settings, group size is up to 20 children (can vary depending on indoor space) and 
the child-staff ratio is 18:1.  
• 
For pre-primary settings under the Ministry of Family and Social Sciences group size for 0-24 months 
is up to 10 and group size for 25-66 months is up to 20.  
5.2. Coverage of 
curriculum frameworks  
Curriculum frameworks prepared for different ECEC programmes are compulsory.

Sources 
Enrolment rates 
Data on enrolment rates (Indicator 1.1) are drawn from the 2024 UNESCO/OECD/Eurostat (UOE) data 
collection on education systems, conducted annually by the United Nations Educational, Scientific and 
Cultural Organization (UNESCO); the OECD; and Eurostat for all OECD Member and Partner countries, 
and published in Education at a Glance 2025 (OECD, 2025[1]). Data for Quebec (Canada), the Flemish 
Community of Belgium, and Morocco were collected for this report through members of the Informal Group 
on TALIS Starting Strong from the respective countries and subnational entities. 
Working conditions 
Data on statutory salaries (Indicator 2.1) come from the 2024 Joint Eurydice-OECD data collection on 
salaries of teachers and school heads, as published in Education at a Glance 2025 (OECD, 2025[1]). For 
the purposes of that data collection, teachers are the individuals with the most responsibility for a group of 
children at the classroom or playroom level and may be referred as pedagogues, educators or childcare 
practitioners.  
Statutory salaries are reported for teachers with the most prevalent qualification after ten years of 
experience in their career, in national currencies and converted into USD using purchasing power parities 
(PPPs) for private consumption from the OECD National Accounts database. The gap in statutory salaries 
between pre-primary and primary teachers after ten years of experience (Indicator 2.2) was calculated for 
this report based on the same source. Data for the Canadian provinces of New Brunswick and Quebec 
were collected for this report through members of the Informal Group on TALIS Starting Strong from 
New Brunswick and Quebec, reported in national currencies, and converted into USD using PPPs for 
private consumption, reference year 2023, as provided by Education at a Glance 2025 (see Table X2.8) 
(OECD, 2025[2]). 
Data on teachers’ total statuary working time (Indicator 2.3) and statuary net teaching time (Indicator 2.4) 
come from the biennial system-level data collection of the INES NESLI Network on teaching and working 
time of teachers as published in Education at a Glance 2024 (OECD, 2024[3]). Sources of the data provided 
by the national and subnational representatives for the INES NESLI Network data collection are presented 
in Table X3.D4.1 (OECD, 2024[3]). 
Expenditure and funding 
Data on expenditure on early childhood education and care (ECEC) as a share of gross domestic product 
(GDP) (Indicator 3.1), expenditure on ECEC per child (Indicator 3.2), the share of private expenditure 
(Indicator 3.3) and the local government share of public expenditure after inter-governmental transfers 
(Indicator 3.4) are drawn from the 2024 UOE data collection (OECD, 2025[1]). For integrated International 
Standard Classification of Education (ISCED) 01 and 02 systems, expenditure is allocated between levels 
using national methods or, where unavailable, relative group sizes.  
The indicator on expenditure on ECEC (Indicator 3.1) includes both public and private sources, covering 
current and capital expenditure on ECEC institutions. The indicator is calculated by dividing total 
expenditure on ECEC institutions by GDP in the same reference year, expressed as a percentage. 
Expenditure data are reported in national currencies and converted into USD using PPPs for GDP from 
the OECD National Accounts database for international comparability. For the Flemish Community of 
Belgium, expenditure on ECEC was provided by the members of the Informal Group on TALIS Starting 
Strong. Expenditure on ECEC for Quebec (Canada) was provided by the members of the Informal Group 
on TALIS Starting Strong for the period April 2023-March 2024. To calculate expenditure as a share of 

278    
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
GDP, the provincial GDP was sourced from national statistics and averaged across 2023 and 2024 
(Statistics Canada, 2025[4]). The expenditure figure was then divided by the average GDP for these two 
years. 
The indicator on ECEC expenditure per child (Indicator 3.2) is calculated by dividing total expenditure on 
ECEC institutions by the number of children enrolled at the corresponding level of education (OECD, 
2025[1]). Expenditure figures are reported in national currencies and converted into USD using PPPs for 
GDP; enrolment data refer to the same reference year to ensure consistency. For the Flemish Community 
of Belgium, expenditure on ECEC per child was provided by the members of the Informal Group on TALIS 
Starting Strong. For Quebec (Canada), expenditure on ECEC per child was provided by members of the 
Informal Group on TALIS Starting Strong and converted into USD using PPPs for private consumption 
reference year 2023 as provided by Education at a Glance 2025 (see Table X2.8) (OECD, 2025[2]). 
The indicator on the share of private expenditure (Indicator 3.3) refers to the proportion of total spending 
on ECEC institutions derived from private sources, mainly household payments for tuition, ancillary 
services and other private contributions (OECD, 2025[1]). It is calculated by dividing total private 
expenditure by the sum of public and private expenditure on ECEC institutions at each ISCED level, 
expressed as a percentage. The indicator on the local government share (Indicator 3.4) measures the 
proportion of public expenditure financed directly by local authorities after accounting for transfers from 
higher levels of government. For the Flemish Community of Belgium, the share of private expenditure and 
the local government share was provided by the members of the Informal Group on TALIS Starting Strong. 
Data for the indicator on the fee regulations and targeted support (Indicator 3.5) were collected for this 
report through members of the Informal Group on TALIS Starting Strong from the respective countries and 
subnational entities. 
Governance, quality standards and workforce development 
Data for the indicators on governance (Indicators 4.1 and 4.2), quality standards (Indicators 5.1 and 5.2) 
and workforce development (Indicator 6.2) are primarily drawn from Starting Strong VI: Supporting 
Meaningful Interactions in Early Childhood Education and Care and the accompanying Starting Strong: 
Mapping Quality in ECEC database, developed by the OECD between 2018 and 2021 (OECD, 2021[5]; 
2021[6]). For the purposes of this report, the data were recirculated to countries and subnational entities for 
verification, completion of missing information and updates to reflect recent policy developments. Countries 
that did not participate in the original data collection, including Colombia, Morocco, Spain and Sweden, 
provided system-level information specifically for the purposes of this report. 
Data for minimum initial educational attainment required for ECEC classroom/playroom teachers 
(Indicator 6.1) are drawn from the OECD Education at a Glance ECEC Dashboard, which compiles 
information from the ISCED questionnaire (ISCMAP) administered by the UOE data collection (OECD, 
2025[1]). For the purposes of this report, the data were circulated again to countries and subnational entities 
for verification and to provide qualification requirements for non-ISCED programmes that are beyond the 
scope of the ISCMAP questionnaire. Countries and subnational entities that did not participate in the 
original data collection, including Morocco and the Canadian provinces of New Brunswick and Quebec, 
provided information on the qualification requirements specifically for the purposes of this report.

Annex B. Technical notes on sampling, response 
rates and adjudication 
Sampling procedures  
The objective of the Starting Strong Teaching and Learning International Survey (TALIS Starting Strong) 
2024 was to obtain a representative sample in each participating country or subnational entity of staff and 
leaders providing early childhood education and care (ECEC) for each level of ECEC (i.e. ISCED level 02 
or settings for children under age 3) in which the country or subnational entity participated. The international 
sampling plan used a stratified two-stage probability sampling design. ECEC settings served as the 
first-stage units (primary sampling units), randomly selected from within strata. For countries and 
subnational entities with age-integrated ECEC systems that participated in the data collection for both 
pre-primary education (ISCED level 02) and settings for children under age 3, settings serving both age 
groups were randomly split between the two samples so that each setting could be selected for 
participation in only one level of ECEC. Staff were then randomly selected from the list of eligible staff 
within those settings, serving as secondary sampling units. A more detailed description of the survey 
design and its implementation can be found in the TALIS 2024 Technical Report (OECD, forthcoming[1]). 
Participating countries and subnational entities had the option to restrict the coverage of their national 
implementation of TALIS Starting Strong for reasons of practicality, safety or economy. However, they 
were encouraged to minimise such exclusions and ensure that their national survey population covered at 
least 95% of ECEC staff. National project managers were required to document the reasons for each 
exclusion. 
Definition of early childhood education and care settings, leaders and staff  
The international target population of TALIS Starting Strong restricts the survey to those staff and leaders 
who work in officially registered settings providing ECEC. Settings exclusively for children with special 
educational needs are deemed out of scope. For the ISCED level 02 target population, home-based 
settings (i.e. within the homes of the respective staff) are not considered to be part of the target population. 
Officially registered home-based settings are considered in-scope for the target population of settings for 
children under age 3. 
Staff in pre-primary settings (ISCED level 02) include all persons working regularly in a pedagogical way, 
providing learning opportunities or care, with children of ISCED level 02. Staff for children under age 3 
include all persons working regularly in a pedagogical way, providing learning opportunities or care, with 
children in this age group. Staff who provide learning opportunities for both target populations in their 
settings are included in the TALIS Starting Strong population for the age group for which their setting is 
sampled. There is no minimum cut-off for how much time staff need to be engaged at either level of ECEC. 
The following types of staff are considered out of scope for TALIS Starting Strong 2024: temporary 
substitute educators (to replace staff on sick leave), staff on long-term leave (e.g. disability, sabbatical) 
who are not expected to be back working at the time of data collection, persons working as nannies or in 
informal arrangements, staff providing services exclusively within a child’s home (e.g. home visiting), 

   281 
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
volunteer persons who occasionally come in for a special activity, and auxiliary staff (e.g. cleaners, cooking 
staff, etc.) who do not interact regularly in a pedagogical manner with the children. 
The leader at each setting (i.e. the person with the most responsibility for administrative, managerial and/or 
pedagogical leadership at the ECEC setting) was automatically selected for participation. At the time of 
sampling, if the leader was designated as also having staff duties (i.e. meeting the sampling definition of 
ECEC staff), s/he was assigned to participate using the main version of the combined questionnaire (CQM) 
in countries and subnational entities that used this version of the questionnaire (OECD, forthcoming[2]). At 
the time of sampling, if the leader was the only staff member listed in an ECEC setting, s/he was assigned 
to participate using the short version of the combined questionnaire (CQS) in countries and subnational 
entities that used this version of the questionnaire. 
Sample size requirements 
For each level of ECEC, the minimum sample size was set at 180 ECEC settings and 1 200 staff members 
in total. In ECEC settings with eight or more eligible staff members, at least eight staff members were to 
be selected; in settings with fewer eligible staff members, all of them were to be asked to participate.  
Participating countries and subnational entities could choose to augment their samples by selecting more 
settings, or by selecting more staff within each selected setting, or by increasing both. In some cases, 
because the average number of staff in ECEC settings was low, the number of settings sampled was 
increased. 
Adjudication process 
Adjudication is the process of determining whether the data released were “fit-for-use” as intended. The 
issues examined concerned, among others, adapting the questionnaire to national context, translation and 
verification, quality of the sampling frame, handling of out-of-scope and refusal units (i.e. staff and/or 
setting), within‐setting sampling, data collection, data cleaning, the reports of quality observers, 
participation rates and overall compliance with the technical standards.  
During the adjudication session, each individual dataset (i.e. one per level of ECEC per country or 
subnational entity) was submitted to the same examination. Leader/setting participation was adjudicated 
independently of staff data. 
Once each survey process had been assessed, a recommended adjudication rating was formulated. While 
the rating was mostly dictated by the participation rate thresholds set in the TALIS 2024 Technical 
Standards (see Table A B.1 and Table A B.2), the adjudication committee at times improved the rating of 
a dataset if, after expert consideration, unique and favourable conditions were met (e.g. closeness to the 
threshold value and/or the non-response bias analysis report showing evidence for negligible bias risks). 
To note, the adjudication rating is made at the ECEC system level. However, participation rates are just 
one potential source of non-response bias and potential non-response bias is specific to each statistical 
estimate from the survey data. Therefore, some estimates of systems with an adjudication rating of “good” 
might still have a high non-response bias; correspondingly, some estimates of systems with an adjudication 
rating of “poor” might still be reliable. For example, if staff with a particular characteristic systematically do 
not respond to a specific question in a system with a “good” rating, but that question is answered by all 
staff in a system with a “poor” rating, estimates based on that question may be more affected by 
non-response bias in the “good” system compared to the “poor” system. For more detailed information, 
please refer to the TALIS 2024 Technical Report (OECD, forthcoming[1]). 

282    
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
At its January 2025 meeting in Paris, the TALIS 2024 Technical Advisory Group (TAG) recommended that 
data from all participating countries and subnational entities be weighted and displayed in tables, 
regardless of the adjudication ratings. The TAG further recommended clearly indicating to readers when 
data were rated as “insufficient,” due to the greater risk of non-response bias in these samples. TALIS 
Starting Strong 2024 data from New Zealand were deemed an exception to this general principle, although 
the TAG supported presenting these data with the caveat that they are not representative of the population 
targeted by the survey.  
Based on these recommendations, countries and subnational entities with “insufficient” ratings are flagged 
(*) in tables and figures with the note: Estimates should be interpreted with caution due to a higher risk of 
non-response bias. Data from New Zealand are visually separated in tables and figures and are flagged 
(**) in tables, figures and the text with the note: Data only represent respondents included in the sample 
and not the population targeted by the survey. No distinctions are made in the report for data rated as 
“good”, “fair” or “poor”; more detailed implications of these ratings are described in the TALIS 2024 
Technical Report (OECD, forthcoming[1]).

Notes regarding use and interpretation of the data 
This section lists issues to be noted regarding the sampling or field operations that should be considered 
when interpreting the data reported for the following countries and subnational entities: 
• 
Flemish Community of Belgium 
o 
For settings for children under age 3, settings without subsidy were excluded after the survey 
administration due to very low participation rates. They are not considered in the participation 
rate but contribute to the exclusion rate, which remained below 5% of staff. 
• 
Germany 
o 
Home-based settings were excluded from the sample for settings for children under age 3, 
leading to an estimated exclusion rate of 6.5% of staff at this level of ECEC.  
• 
Ireland 
o 
For both levels of ECEC, replacement settings were contacted before the participation of the 
originally sampled setting was defined. Consequently, replacements participated although the 
original setting participated as well. These replacements were moved into separate strata and 
treated as self-representing. They do not contribute to participation rates. 
o 
Home-based settings were excluded from the sample for settings for children under age 3. The 
exclusion rate remained below 5% of staff. 
 
 

286    
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
• 
Israel 
o 
Ultra-Orthodox settings did not participate in the survey at ISCED level 02 and were excluded 
after the survey administration. They are not considered in the participation rate but contribute 
to the exclusion rate, leading to an exclusion rate of 26.3% of pre-primary settings; the resulting 
staff exclusion rate is unknown.  
• 
Morocco  
o 
Due to the very small size of ECEC settings (i.e. there are just one or two staff members in 
many settings), the sample size requirement for staff was reduced. 
• 
New Zealand 
o 
Te Kōhanga Reo (Māori language immersion environments for children and families) did not 
participate in the survey at ISCED level 02, leading to an estimated staff exclusion rate of 12.8% 
for pre-primary staff. For settings for children under age 3, in addition to Te Kōhanga Reo, free 
kindergartens and playcentres did not participate in the survey, leading to an overall estimated 
staff exclusion rate of 29.8%. 
• 
Norway 
o 
Home-based settings were excluded from the sample for settings for children under age 3. The 
exclusion rate remained below 5% of staff. 
• 
Quebec (Canada) 
o 
Due to a high turnover in home-based childcare services during the time of recruitment for 
TALIS Starting Strong, notably attributable to demographic factors such as retirement, it is 
important to note that results for leaders/settings represent settings that were open in 
September 2022 and remained open at the beginning of 2024: At the time of data collection, 
32% of home-based settings that had been selected for the sample were ineligible, mainly 
because they were closed.

Annex C. Technical notes on analyses in this 
report 
Use of staff and setting weights 
The statistics presented in this report were derived from data obtained through samples of settings, setting 
leaders and staff (see Annex B). For these statistics to describe a country or subnational entity they need 
to reflect the whole population from which they were drawn and not merely the sample used to collect 
them. Survey weights must therefore be used to obtain design-unbiased estimates of population or model 
parameters. 
Final weights allow the production of country- or subnational entity-level estimates from the observed 
sample data. The estimation weight indicates how many population units are represented by a sampled 
unit. The final weight is the combination of many factors reflecting the probabilities of selection at the 
various stages of sampling and the response obtained at each stage. Other factors may also come into 
play as dictated by special conditions to maintain the unbiasedness of the estimates (e.g. adjustment for 
staff working in more than one centre). A detailed description of the sampling and weighting procedures 
can be found in the TALIS Starting Strong 2024 Technical Report (OECD, forthcoming[1]). 
Data from New Zealand were weighted differently. Due to sparse response that led to unanticipated 
exclusions for some segments of the target population these samples (both ISCED 02 and settings for 
children under age 3) cannot be adequately adjusted through weighting to represent early childhood 
education and care (ECEC) settings and staff in New Zealand overall. Nonetheless, staff and setting 
weights were applied to adjust for different probabilities of selection into the sample, but not to make the 
samples representative of the overall target population for New Zealand. Thus, estimates presented in this 
volume for New Zealand only represent respondents included in the sample and not the population 
targeted by the survey. 
Statistics in this report that represent settings and statistics based solely on leader responses were 
estimated using leader weights (LEADERWGT). Leader weights are used to make the sample 
representative of the population of ECEC settings in each country and subnational entity (with the 
exception of New Zealand); results are not representative of the population of leaders. Results that 
represent staff, which are based only on responses of staff or on responses of staff and leaders 
(i.e. responses from setting leaders were merged with staff responses), were estimated using staff weights 
(STAFFWGT). Staff weights are used to make the sample representative of the population of ECEC staff 
in each country and subnational entity (with the exception of New Zealand). 
Coverage of the sample 
Not all respondents answer every question, either because they omit a response or give an invalid 
response. Coverage is defined as the weighted percentage of respondents who provide a valid answer to 
a given question in the survey, regardless of their answer. Coverage daggers are shown in every table to 
indicate the level of coverage: no symbol means at least 75% of the expected population was covered; 

288    
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
one dagger (†) means at least 50% but less than 75%; and a double-dagger (‡) means less than 50% was 
covered. 
Coverage is calculated based on the section of the sample that could answer the relevant question in the 
survey. Some respondents did not have the possibility of answering some questions due to filters in the 
questionnaire (i.e. the question being not logically applicable) or to use of different versions of the 
questionnaire targeted to different types of setting leaders and staff (see the TALIS Starting Strong 2024 
Technical Report for more detail (OECD, forthcoming[1]). These respondents were excluded from coverage 
and estimate calculations. In addition, respondents in home-based settings were excluded from coverage 
and estimate calculations wherever the analysis was not appropriate for this type of setting. These 
exclusions are indicated in notes below the relevant tables in Annex D. 
Standard errors and significance tests 
Standard errors 
The statistics in this report represent estimates based on samples of staff and settings, rather than values 
that could be calculated if every staff member and setting leader in every country or subnational entity had 
answered every question. Consequently, it is important to measure the degree of uncertainty of the 
estimates. In the Starting Strong Teaching and Learning International Survey (TALIS Starting Strong) 
2024, each estimate has an associated degree of uncertainty that is expressed through a standard error. 
The reported standard errors were computed with a Balanced Repeated Replication (BRR) method, which 
provides a simple way of producing a consistent estimator that takes into account the sampling strategies 
used in the survey (a detailed description can be found in the TALIS Starting Strong 2024 Technical Report 
(OECD, forthcoming[1]). The use of confidence intervals provides a way to make inferences about the 
population statistics in a manner that reflects the uncertainty associated with the sample estimates. From 
an observed sample statistic and assuming a normal distribution, its corresponding confidence interval at 
the 95% level can be constructed as the estimated parameter, plus or minus 1.96 times the associated 
standard error. In this way, it can be inferred that the corresponding parameter for the population would lie 
within the confidence interval in 95 out of 100 replications of the measurement on different samples drawn 
from the same population. 
Differences between sub-groups 
Differences between sub-groups either for staff (e.g. teachers and assistants) or setting characteristics 
(e.g. settings with a high concentration of children from socio-economically disadvantaged homes and 
settings with a low concentration of children from socio-economically disadvantaged homes) were tested 
for statistical significance. All differences marked in bold in the data tables of this report are statistically 
significantly different from 0 at the 95% level. This means that differences were labelled as statistically 
significant when a difference of that size or greater, in either direction, would be observed less than 5% of 
the time in the sample estimates, if there were actually no difference in the corresponding population 
values. In the case of differences between sub-groups, standard errors were calculated by taking into 
account that the two sub-samples are not independent, with the help of the BRR method. 
Statistics based on regressions 
Country- and subnational entity-specific regression analyses were performed to examine the associations 
between different variables. TALIS Starting Strong 2024 used a multivariable regression model for linear 
and logistic regression analyses. The primary goal was to accurately estimate the independent contribution 
of each predictor while adjusting for potential confounders. This model allows expressing the outcome as 

   289 
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
a function of all relevant variables simultaneously – where each coefficient (or odds ratio, OR) represents 
the expected change in the outcome (or in the likelihood of the outcome rather than the reference category, 
for ORs) when that predictor increases by one unit (or takes the indicated value rather than the reference 
category, for categorical variables), holding all other variables constant. Regression analyses presented in 
this report handle missing data by listwise deletion. The percentage of the sample on which the regression 
is estimated is indicated by the coverage signs presented in the tables (see above for details). 
Two types of models were used in this volume. Multiple linear regression was used in those cases where 
the dependent (or outcome) variable was considered continuous, such as scales. Binary logistic regression 
was employed when the dependent (or outcome) variable was a binary categorical variable, such as a 
high versus a low share of children from socio-economically disadvantaged homes. 
Linear regression models 
Linear regression models were used where the dependent (or outcome) variable was continuous. The 
models were specified as follows: 
𝑌= 𝛽0 + 𝛽1𝑋1 + 𝛽2𝑋2 + ⋯+ 𝛽𝑝𝑋𝑝+ 𝜖 
Where Y is the predicted variable, 𝑋1, 𝑋2, … , 𝑋p is the set of regressors, 𝛽1, 𝛽2, … , 𝛽p is the set of the 
coefficients that measure the strength of association, and 𝜖 is an error term. Coefficients presented in the 
tables are unstandardised, were estimated using the Ordinary Least Squares method and represent the 
expected change in the Y variable with a change in the predictor, holding all other predictors constant. The 
magnitude of the unstandardised coefficient linking a predictor and an outcome in a linear regression is 
referred to as the “strength of association.” If the predictor is a categorical or binary variable, the coefficient 
represents the change in Y associated with being in the specified category rather than the reference 
category. If the predictor is a continuous variable, the coefficient represents the change in Y associated 
with a one-unit change in the predictor. Associated standard errors were calculated using the BRR method. 
Coefficients were tested for difference from 0 at the 95% confidence level using a Student’s t-test. 
Coefficients are indicated to be statistically significantly different from 0 when an estimate of that size or 
greater, in either direction, would be observed less than 5% of the time in the sample estimates, if the 
corresponding population value were actually 0. 
Logistic regression models (odds ratios) 
Logistic regression (logit) models were used where the dependent (or outcome) variable was binary. The 
models were specified as follows: 
𝐿𝑜𝑔𝑖𝑡(𝜋(𝑦)) = ln (
𝜋(𝑦)
1 −𝜋(𝑦)) = 𝛽0 + 𝛽1𝑋1 + 𝛽2𝑋2 + ⋯+ 𝛽𝑝𝑋𝑝+ 𝜖 
Where Y is the predicted variable, with probability 𝜋(𝑦) for outcome y instead of the reference category, 
𝑋1, 𝑋2, … , 𝑋p is the set of regressors, 𝛽1, 𝛽2, … , 𝛽p is the set of the coefficients that measure the strength of 
association, and 𝜖 is an error term. Associated standard errors were calculated using the BRR method. 
In the tables, logit coefficients are not reported directly. Instead, ORs are presented. The OR is a measure 
of the relative likelihood of a particular outcome across two groups. In this case, ORs are equivalent to the 
ratio between the probability of observing outcome y instead of the reference category when a predictor 
takes on a particular value instead of the reference category, and the probability of observing the same 
outcome in Y when the predictor does not take that value. Given that 𝑝𝑋𝑌 is the probability of observing a 
given outcome for Y given a particular value for X, the OR can be defined as follows: 
𝑂𝑅= 𝑝11 𝑝12
⁄
𝑝21 𝑝22
⁄
 

290    
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
Where 𝑝11 𝑝12
⁄
 represents the “odds” of observing the indicated outcome (1) rather than the reference 
category (2) in Y, when X takes on its indicated outcome (1); and 𝑝21 𝑝22
⁄
 represents the “odds” of 
observing the outcome (1) rather than the reference category (2) in Y, when X takes on the value of its 
reference category (2).  
If the predictor is a categorical or binary, the OR represents the change in likelihood of Y taking on value 
y rather than the reference category associated when the predictor takes the specified value rather than 
its reference category. If the predictor is continuous, the coefficient represents the change in the likelihood 
of Y taking on value y associated with a one-unit change in the predictor. 
In this report, the OR is obtained by applying an exponential transformation to the estimated logit 
coefficient. Throughout the report, ORs were calculated as the exponential of the logit coefficient, so that 
for any regressor j and its associated coefficient 𝛽j, its OR is: 
𝑂𝑅𝑗= 𝑒𝛽𝑗 
Since this transformation is non-linear, statistical inference is performed on the original coefficient rather 
than on the OR itself. Hypothesis tests (Student’s t-tests) use the coefficient estimates and their standard 
errors. P-values are computed directly from the original coefficients and standard errors, using the inverse 
of the normal cumulative distribution function. This ensures accurate statistical inference and avoids errors 
that may arise from testing transformed values like ORs. 
The same procedure can be applied to all predictors individually, with no modifications needed for each 
variable. The resulting p-value reflects the probability of observing an estimate of that size or greater, in 
either direction, if the true population logit coefficient were 0 (or the corresponding population OR were 1). 
In this report, ORs are indicated as statistically significant when an estimate of their associated logit 
coefficient of that size or greater, in either direction, would be observed less than 5% of the time in the 
sample estimates, if the corresponding population value for the logit coefficient were actually 0. This is 
equivalent to an associated p-value of less than 0.05. 
Predictors used in all regressions 
For each regression model, a consistent set of predictors (detailed below) and a more restricted set of 
predictors specific to the analytical goals of each model were included. The selection of predictors was 
grounded in substantive theory and prior research. This helped avoid overfitting and ensured that the 
included predictors have a meaningful interpretation. Multicollinearity was assessed by estimating 
bivariable variance inflation factors on the set of predictors used in all regressions. This analysis confirmed 
that the model’s independent effects are estimable with acceptable precision. 
Predictors in all staff models 
• 
Level of formal education: Based on the highest level of formal education completed by staff 
(variable: SS2G11). It was grouped into three categories: secondary education or below (ISCED 
level 3 or below), post-secondary non-tertiary or short-cycle tertiary education (ISCED levels 4 or 
5), and bachelor’s degree or equivalent or higher (ISCED level 6 or higher; used as the reference 
category). 
• 
Experience: Based on total experience as ECEC staff (variable: SS2G08B). It was grouped into 
three categories: 3 years or less, between 4 and 9 years, and 10 years or more (used as the 
reference category). 
• 
Employment status: Based on the contract agreement staff have to work at their ECEC setting 
(variable: SS2G05). It was grouped into two categories: permanent employment and others (which 

   291 
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
encompasses fixed-term, self-employed or no employment contract; used as the reference 
category). 
• 
Working hours: Based on working hours of ECEC staff (variable: SS2G06). It was grouped into 
two categories: full-time and part-time (whether staff indicated working 50-90% of full-time hours 
or less than 50% of full-time hours; used as the reference category). 
• 
Staff role: Based on the category that staff indicated best represents their role in general when 
working at their ECEC setting (variable: SS2G03). It was grouped into two categories: teacher or 
ECEC setting leader and all other staff roles (which encompasses assistants, staff for individual 
children, staff for special tasks, interns and others; used as the reference category). 
• 
Setting location: Based on the ECEC setting’s location (variable: SL2G13). It was grouped into 
two categories: a municipality with more than 15 000 people and a municipality with 15 000 people 
or less (used as the reference category). 
• 
Setting type of management: Based on the organisation responsible for the day-to-day 
management of the ECEC setting (variable: SL2G19). It comprises two categories: publicly 
managed and privately managed (used as the reference category). 
• 
Setting size (or target group size) tertiles: The position of the ECEC setting in the national 
distribution of ECEC setting size, based on the number of children enrolled in the ECEC setting 
(variable: SL2G25) at the setting level, or on the number of children in the target group (variable: 
SS2G35) at the target group level (relevant tables specify whether the setting size or target group 
size is used). Depending on the outcome, this variable refers either to the number of children 
enrolled in the ECEC setting or the number of children in the target group. The first [bottom] tertile 
was used as the reference category. Coefficients are therefore a comparison to the smallest ECEC 
settings (or target groups) in each country or subnational entity. 
• 
Number of staff per child in the setting (or target group) tertiles: The position of the ECEC 
setting in the national distribution of number of staff per children, based on the sum of staff in the 
setting (based on variable: SL2G22) and the number of children enrolled in the setting (variable: 
SL2G25) at the setting level, or on the sum of staff in the target group (based on variable: SS2G34) 
and the number of children in the target group (variable: SS2G35) at the target group level (relevant 
tables specify whether the number of staff per child in the setting or target group is used). 
Depending on the outcome, this variable refers either to the number of staff per children in the 
ECEC setting or the number of staff per children in the target group. The first [bottom] tertile was 
used as the reference category. Coefficients are therefore a comparison to the ECEC settings (or 
target groups) where there are the fewest number of staff per child in each country or subnational 
entity. 
Predictors in all setting/leader models 
• 
Level of formal education: Based on the highest level of formal education completed by leaders 
(variable: SL2G03). It was grouped into three categories: short-cycle tertiary education or below 
(ISCED level 5 or below), bachelor’s degree or equivalent (ISCED level 6), and master’s degree or 
equivalent or higher (ISCED level 7 or higher; used as the reference category). 
• 
Experience: Based on total experience as an ECEC setting leader (variable: SL2G05B). It was 
grouped into three categories: 3 years or less, between 4 and 9 years, and 0 years or more (used 
as the reference category). 
• 
Setting location: Based on the ECEC setting’s location (variable: SL2G13). It was grouped into 
two categories: a municipality with more than 15 000 people and a municipality with 15 000 people 
or less (used as the reference category). 

292    
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
• 
Setting type of management: Based on the organisation responsible for the day-to-day 
management of the ECEC setting (variable: SL2G19). It comprises two categories: publicly 
managed and privately managed (used as the reference category). 
• 
Setting size tertiles: The position of the ECEC setting in the national distribution of ECEC setting 
size, based on the number of children enrolled in the ECEC setting (variable: SL2G25) at the setting 
level. For ECEC setting/leader outcomes, this variable refers to the number of children enrolled in 
the ECEC setting. The first [bottom] tertile was used as the reference category. Coefficients are 
therefore a comparison to the smallest ECEC settings in each country or subnational entity. 
• 
Number of staff per child in the setting tertiles: The position of the ECEC setting in the national 
distribution of number of staff per children, based on the sum of staff in the setting (based on 
variable: SL2G22) and the number of children enrolled in the setting (variable: SL2G25) at the 
setting level. For ECEC setting/leader outcomes, this variable refers to the number of staff per child 
in the ECEC setting. The first [bottom] tertile was used as the reference category. Coefficients are 
therefore a comparison to the ECEC settings where there are the fewest number of staff per child 
in each country or subnational entity. 
Use of complex variables 
Setting (or target group) size 
Tertiles refer to 3 groups comprised approximately between the 1st and 33rd percentiles (first [bottom] 
tertile), the 34th and the 66th percentiles, and the 67th and 100th percentiles, in the weighted distribution 
of setting (or target group) size. Setting size is the number of children enrolled in the ECEC setting 
(variable: SL2G25). Target group size is the number of children in the target group (variable: SS2G35). 
Tertiles were calculated within each country and subnational entity, meaning that the values that go into 
each percentile vary with each country and subnational entity. These tertiles are of unequal size but of 
equal value, meaning that they group all observations with the same value in the same bin (typically the 
lower one). Therefore, the number of (weighted) observations in each bin is different, but all observations 
with the same value of setting (or target group) size are grouped in the same bin. For the setting size 
tertiles in Quebec (Canada), a further recoding was done, grouping all settings with less than 10 children 
enrolled, and dividing the rest of the settings (ranging from 29 to 191 enrolled children) into 2 similar-size 
groups. This procedure creates “tertiles” that are of very unequal (weighted) size, but have a clear cut in 
values, fit for analytical purposes. 
Number of staff per child in the setting (or target group) 
Tertiles refer to 3 groups comprised approximately between the 1st and 33rd percentiles, the 34th and the 
66th percentiles, and the 67th and 100th percentiles, in the weighted distribution of staff per child in the 
setting (or in the target group). Staff per child in the setting is the number of staff working in the setting 
(sum of items A-D of variable SL2G22) divided by the number of children enrolled in the ECEC setting 
(variable: SL2G25). Staff per child in the target group is the number of staff in the target group (the sum of 
items A-D of variable SS2G34) divided by the number of children in the target group (variable: SS2G35). 
Tertiles were calculated within each country and subnational entity, meaning that the values that go into 
each percentile vary with each country and subnational entity. These tertiles are of unequal size but of 
equal value, meaning that they group all observations with the same value in the same bin (typically the 
lower one). Therefore, the number of (weighted) observations in each bin is different, but all observations 
with the same value of setting (or target group) size are grouped in the same bin. 

   293 
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
Proportions of children with various characteristics in the setting (or target group) 
Proportion variables were created to represent the share of children with specific characteristics (eight 
different characteristics captured in SL2G27a-h) relative to the total number of children in the setting 
(SL2G25) or the share of children with specific characteristics (five different characteristics captured in 
SS2G36a-e) relative to the total number of children in the target group (SS2G35). Each proportion was 
calculated as (SL2G27x/SL2G25) × 100 for the setting and (SS2G36x/SS2G35) × 100 for the target group. 
Values resulting from division by zero or exceeding 125% were set to missing. The threshold of 125% was 
chosen instead of 100% because, during the cleaning process, the interrelation between the pairs of 
variables was intentionally relaxed to retain as many valid values as possible. Categorical dummy variables 
were then generated from the proportions, coded as 0 for less than 10% and 1 for 10% or more. Specific 
characteristics could overlap, meaning a child could be counted in more than one category. 
Staff working hours and reasons for part-time work 
Staff working hours and reasons for part-time work refers to the current working hours of staff as full time 
or part time (variable: SS2G06) and, in case of part-time work, the reason for part-time work (variable: 
SS2G07). It has three categories: full time, voluntary part time and involuntary part time. An ECEC staff is 
considered to be working full time when they indicated full-time working hours in SS2G06. An ECEC staff 
is considered to be voluntarily working part time when they indicated either part time in SS2G06 or their 
answer was omitted or invalid, and answered they were only looking for part-time work in SS2G07. An 
ECEC staff is considered to be involuntarily working part time when they indicated either part time in 
SS2G06 or their answer was omitted or invalid, and answered they were only offered part-time work in 
SS2G07. 
Type of management and profit status 
The categorical variable “type of management and profit status” was derived to represent the combined 
classification of management type and profit status for each setting (SL2G19 and SL2G21). Three 
categories were generated: publicly managed settings, privately managed for-profit settings and privately 
managed non-profit settings. Settings were classified as publicly managed cases regardless of their profit 
status if the leader indicated public management in SL2G19. 
Use of scales 
Several scales are used in this report, in particular in regression analyses. TALIS Starting Strong 2024 
scales were constructed using the same international model parameters to be statistically equivalent 
across countries and subnational entities, facilitating scale score comparability across ECEC systems. In 
general, scale scores created for TALIS Starting Strong 2024 can be used in different types of analyses, 
including comparisons of country and subnational entity means for each individual scale. 
TALIS Starting Strong 2024 scale scores are standardised to have a standard deviation of 2 across all 
education systems participating in TALIS Starting Strong 2024. A value of 10 corresponds to the item 
mid-point value of the response scale. A detailed description of the construction and validation of TALIS 
Starting Strong 2024 scales can be found in Chapter 11 of the TALIS 2024 Technical Report (OECD, 
forthcoming[1]). 
Adequacy of physical space 
The scale of “adequacy of physical space” (S2LSQSET; referred to as “Structural quality: Adequacy of 
setting” in the TALIS 2024 Technical Report (OECD, forthcoming[1])) was constructed using leader 

294    
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
responses (“strongly disagree”, “disagree”, “agree”, “strongly agree”) to seven statements which apply to 
their ECEC setting (SL2G28A, SL2G28B, SL2G28C, SL2G28D, SL2G28E, SL2G28F and SL2G28G): 
“There is adequate ventilation for healthy airflow”; “There is an adequate amount of furniture for each child 
(e.g. chairs, tables)”; “Indoor space is arranged so that children can easily move around”; “The background 
noise level in the classroom/playgroup/group does not interfere with interactions”; “The roof, ceiling, walls 
and floor are in good shape (e.g. have no leaks, mould)”; “Children can access a bathroom easily”; and 
“Outdoor space is arranged so that children can easily move around”. Higher scores on this scale indicate 
more positive perceptions of the physical space in the ECEC setting. 
Barriers to structural quality: Adequacy of physical resources 
The scale of “barriers to structural quality: adequacy of physical resources” (S2LSQPRS) was constructed 
using leaders’ responses (“not at all”, “to some extent”, “quite a bit”, “a lot”) to four statements on issues 
with the availability of physical resources that hinder the setting’s capacity to provide a quality environment 
for children’s development, well-being and learning (SL2G42A, SL2G42B, SL2G42C and SL2G42D): 
“Shortage or inadequacy of play or learning materials (e.g. picture books, building blocks, paint)”; 
“Shortage or inadequacy of indoor space”; “Shortage or inadequacy of outdoor play space”; and “Shortage 
or inadequacy of digital resources and tools”. Higher scores on this scale indicate greater perceived 
barriers to structural quality related to the adequacy of physical resources. 
Barriers to structural quality: Adequacy of staffing 
The scale of “barriers to structural quality: adequacy of staffing” (S2LSQSTA) was constructed using 
leaders’ responses (“not at all”, “to some extent”, “quite a bit”, “a lot”) on staffing issues that hinder the 
setting’s capacity to provide a quality environment for children’s development, well-being and learning 
(SL2G42I, SL2G42J, SL2G42K, SL2G42L, SL2G42M, SL2G42N and SL2G42O): “<ECEC staff> 
absences”; “Lack of opportunities or support for <ECEC staff>’s professional development”; “Shortage of 
<ECEC staff> with competence in working with children from <socio-economically disadvantaged 
homes>“; “Shortage of <ECEC staff> with competence in working with children with special education 
needs”; “Shortage of <ECEC staff> with competence in working with children speaking a language other 
than the language(s) used in the <ECEC setting>“; “Shortage of qualified staff”; and “Shortage of staff 
relative to the number of enrolled children”. This scale is available only for pre-primary settings. Higher 
scores on this scale reflect greater perceived barriers to structural quality in relation to staffing adequacy. 
Behavioural support in the classroom/playroom 
The scale of “behavioural support in the classroom/playroom” (S2SOCIALR) was constructed using staff 
responses (“never”, “rarely”, “occasionally”, “often”, “always”) to five statements which apply to their work 
in the target group at their ECEC setting (SS2G41A, SS2G41B, SS2G41C, SS2G41D and SS2G41F): “I 
explained the rules children should follow at the start of a new activity”; “I addressed children’s disruptive 
behaviour by explaining why they should stop”; “I showed appreciation to children that behaved well during 
an activity”; “I helped children understand the consequences if they did not follow the rules”; and “I 
redirected a child to an appropriate activity if they were disturbing other children”. Higher scores on this 
scale reflect a greater level of behavioural support provided in the classroom/playroom.

a week”, “a few times a week”, “once a day”, “more than once a day”) to four statements about how 
frequently they used the following practices in their work during the most recent complete calendar week 
(SS2G32D,

allow children to take the initiative”; “Involved children in making plans for the day”; and “Adapted daily 
activities in response to children’s natural rhythms (e.g. hunger, fatigue)”. Higher scores on this scale 
indicate a greater frequency of use of child-centred approaches to planning. 
Daily adaptive pedagogical practices 
The scale of “daily adaptive pedagogical practices” (S2PRACTR) was constructed using staff responses 
(“never”, “rarely”, “occasionally”, “often”, “always”) to five statements which apply to their work in the target 
group at their ECEC setting (SS2G40A, SS2G40B, SS2G40C, SS2G40E and SS2G40F): “I explained how 
a new activity relates to children’s lives”; “I gave different activities to suit different children’s interests”; “I 
gave different activities to suit different children’s levels of development”; “I recognised children’s individual 
interests regardless of gender”; and “I adapted my activities to meet the needs of dual/second language 
learners”. Higher values on this scale reflect greater use of daily adaptive pedagogical practices. 
Distributed leadership 
The scale of “distributed leadership” (S2LDLEAD) was constructed using leaders’ responses (“not at all”, 
“to some extent”, “quite a bit”, “a lot”) to six statements on leadership practices (SL2G41A, SL2G41B, 
SL2G41C, SL2G41D, SL2G41F and SL2G41G): “<ECEC staff> have opportunities to actively participate 
in <ECEC setting> decisions”; “Parents or guardians have opportunities to actively participate in <ECEC 
setting> decisions”; “Children have opportunities to actively participate in decisions”; “There is a culture of 
shared responsibility for <ECEC setting> issues”; “There is a collaborative <ECEC setting> culture which 
is characterised by mutual support”; and “This <ECEC setting> encourages staff to lead new initiatives”. 
Higher scores in this scale indicate greater distributed leadership within the ECEC setting. 
Engagement in collaborative practices 
The scale of “engagement in collaborative practices” (S2COLLR) was constructed using staff responses 
(“never”, “less than monthly”, “monthly”, “weekly”, “daily”) to five statements on collaborative practices they 
engage in (SS2G42A, SS2G42B, SS2G42C, SS2G42D and SS2G42F): “Provide feedback to other 
<ECEC staff> about their practice”; “Engage in joint activities across different groups, including age 
groups”; “Exchange learning or pedagogical materials with colleagues”; “Co-design activities or content for 
children’s development, well-being and learning with colleagues”; and “Discuss the evaluation of children’s 
development, well-being and learning with other <ECEC staff>”. Higher values on this scale indicate a 
greater level of engagement in collaborative practices. 
Facilitating emotional development 
The scale of “facilitating emotional development” (S2PREMR) was constructed using staff responses (“not 
at all”, “once a week”, “a few times a week”, “once a day”, “more than once a day”) to three statements 
about how frequently they used the following practices in their work during the most recent complete 
calendar week (SS2G33A, SS2G33B and SS2G33D): “Talked with children about feelings”; “Helped 
children understand their feelings (e.g. when they are excited)”; and “Helped children express their 
feelings”. Higher values on this scale reflect more frequent activities to facilitate the emotional development 
of children. 
Facilitating language development 
The scale of “facilitating language development” (S2PRLANR) was constructed using staff responses 
(“never”, “rarely”, “occasionally”, “often”, “always”) to five statements which apply to their work in the target

group at their ECEC setting (SS2G39A, SS2G39B, SS2G39C, SS2G39D and SS2G39F): “I encouraged

Facilitating literacy development 
The scale of “facilitating literacy development” (S2PROLIR) was constructed using staff responses (“not at

Facilitating numeracy development 
The scale of “facilitating numeracy development” (S2PRONUR) was constructed using staff responses 
(“not at all”, “once a week”, “a few times a week”, “once a day”, “more than once a day”) to five statements 
about how frequently they used the following practices in their work during the most recent complete 
calendar week (SS2G30A, SS2G30B, SS2G30C, SS2G30D and SS2G30F): “Used sorting activities by 
shape or colour”; “Played number games”; “Sang songs with numbers”; “Helped children to use numbers 
or to count”; and “Encouraged children to measure items (e.g. while cooking or building with blocks)”. 
Higher values on this scale indicate more frequent engagement in activities that support children’s 
numeracy development. 
Facilitating parent/guardian engagement 
The scale of “facilitating parent/guardian engagement” (S2PARENR) was constructed using staff 
responses (“strongly disagree”, “disagree”, “agree”, “strongly agree”) to five statements on parent/guardian 
engagement practices as applied to their ECEC setting (SS2G43A, SS2G43C, SS2G43D, SS2G43E and 
SS2G43F): “Parents or guardians are informed about daily activities on a regular basis”; “Parents or 
guardians are encouraged by <ECEC staff> to participate in regular classroom/playgroup/group activities”; 
“Parents or guardians are encouraged by <ECEC staff> to contribute to classroom/playgroup/group level 
decisions”; “Parents or guardians are encouraged by <ECEC staff> to take on ECEC setting-level tasks”; 
and “Parents or guardians are given opportunities to actively participate in ECEC setting decisions”. Higher 
scores on this scale indicate that <ECEC staff> agree more strongly that parent/guardian engagement is 
supported in their ECEC setting. 
Facilitating play 
The scale of “facilitating play” (S2PRINTR) was constructed using staff responses (“not at all”, “once a

frequently they used the following practices in their work during the most recent complete calendar week 
(SS2G32A, SS2G32B and SS2G32C): “Allowed children to take the lead when I played with them”; 
“Responded positively to non-verbal invitations to play (e.g. a child passing me a toy)”; and “Let a child

play alone if they were deeply engaged in play”. Higher values on this scale reflect more frequent activities

Facilitating prosocial behaviour 
The scale of “facilitating prosocial behaviour” (S2PRSOC) was constructed using staff responses (“not at 
all”, “once a week”, “a few times a week”, “once a day”, “more than once a day”) to four statements about 
how frequently they used the following practices in their work during the most recent complete calendar 
week (SS2G31A, SS2G31B, SS2G31C and SS2G31D): “Encouraged sharing amongst children”; 
“Encouraged children if they helped each other”; “Encouraged children playing in small groups to include 
other children”; and “Encouraged children if they comforted each other”. Higher values on this scale 
indicate a higher frequency of activities that facilitate prosocial behaviour among children. 
Leader innovations in the ECEC setting 
The scale of “leader innovations in the ECEC setting” (S2LEADSPL2) was constructed using leader 
responses (“never”, “less than monthly”, “monthly”, “weekly”, “daily”) to three statements on leadership 
practices they engage in (SL2G32F, SL2G32G and SL2G32H): “I worked on developing, revising or 
communicating a <vision> for this ECEC setting”; “I made changes in <ECEC setting> policies or practices 
based on monitoring results and external evaluation”; and “I facilitated improvements in the indoor or 
outdoor space used for children’s development, well-being and learning”. Higher scores on this scale 
reflect a greater frequency of innovative leadership practices within the ECEC setting. 
Leader support for staff in pedagogical learning 
The scale of “leader support for staff in pedagogical learning” (S2LEADSPL1) was constructed using 
leaders’ responses (“never”, “less than monthly”, “monthly”, “weekly”, “daily”) to four statements on how 
often they engaged in various leadership activities over the past year (SL2G32A, SL2G32B, SL2G32C and 
SL2G32D): “I collaborated with <ECEC staff> to improve how children play together”; “I observed <ECEC 
staff> practices and staff-child interactions”; “I provided feedback to <ECEC staff> based on my 
observations”; and “I took actions to support co-operation among <ECEC staff> to develop new 
approaches to ECEC practices”. Higher scores on this scale indicate more frequent leadership practices 
aimed at supporting the pedagogical learning of ECEC staff. 
Level of stress and its impact on staff well-being 
The scale of “level of stress and its impact on staff well-being” (S2STRESS) was constructed using staff 
responses (“not at all”, “to some extent”, “quite a bit”, “a lot”) to four statements about their experience as 
an <ECEC staff> at their ECEC setting (SS2G46A, SS2G46B, SS2G46C and SS2G46D): “I experience 
stress in my work”; “My job leaves me time for my personal life”; “My job negatively impacts my mental 
health”; and “My job negatively impacts my physical health”. Higher scores reflect greater perceived stress 
(and less perceived well-being).

(“strongly disagree”, “disagree”, “agree”, “strongly agree”) to five statements which apply to their ECEC 
setting (SL2G14A, SL2G14B, SL2G14C, SL2G14D and SL2G14F): “There is litter lying around”; “There is 
vandalism”; “People from diverse backgrounds experience insults or attacks (e.g. different ethnicity or 
culture, migrant background, sexual or gender minorities)”; “There are public places where children can
