play safely”; and “There are drug-related problems”. Higher values on this scale indicate more critical, or 
negative, perceptions of the neighbourhood environment. 
Promoting awareness and acceptance of diversity 
The scale of “promoting awareness and acceptance of diversity” (S2PAAD) was constructed using staff 
responses (“not at all”, “to some extent”, “quite a bit”, “a lot”) on the applicability of five statements to the 
ECEC setting (SS2G23A, SS2G23C, SS2G23D, SS2G23E and SS2G23G): “Books and pictures feature 
people from a variety of ethnic and cultural groups”; “Books and pictures feature people with different 
physical needs”; “Books and pictures feature diverse family structures (e.g. single parent families, families 
with parents from sexual or gender minorities)”; “Children play with toys and artefacts from cultures other 
than the ethnic majority”; and “Activities aim to raise awareness about the different languages of the 
<dual/second language learners> in the <classroom/playgroup/group>“. Higher scores on this scale 
indicate a stronger emphasis on promotion awareness and acceptance of diversity. 
Satisfaction with setting leadership 
The scale of “satisfaction with setting leadership” (S2SSLEAD) was constructed using staff responses 
(“strongly disagree”, “disagree”, “agree”, “strongly agree”) to seven statements on the ECEC setting 
leadership (SS2G44A, SS2G44B, SS2G44C, SS2G44D, SS2G44E, SS2G44F and SS2G44G): “The 
<ECEC setting leader> has a clear vision for this ECEC setting”; “The <ECEC setting leader> encourages 
co-operation among <ECEC staff> to develop new ideas in their practices”; “The <ECEC setting leader> 
encourages <ECEC staff> to take responsibility for improving their practices”; “The <ECEC setting leader> 
encourages all staff to have a say on important decisions”; “The <ECEC setting leader> promotes a culture 
of shared professional learning”; “The <ECEC setting leader> communicates effectively with <ECEC 
staff>“; and “I need more support from my <ECEC setting leader>“. Higher scores on this scale reflect 
greater satisfaction with the leadership at the ECEC setting. 
Satisfaction with work at this ECEC setting 
The scale of “satisfaction with work at this ECEC setting” (S2JOBSAR) was constructed using staff 
responses (“strongly disagree”, “disagree”, “agree”, “strongly agree”) to four statements about their 
experience as an ECEC staff at their ECEC setting (SS2G45C, SS2G45E, SS2G45F and SS2G45G): “I 
enjoy working at this <ECEC setting>”;  “I am satisfied with the support that I receive from parents or 
guardians in this <ECEC setting>“; “I would recommend this <ECEC setting> as a good place to work”; 
and “I would like to change to another <ECEC setting> if that were possible”. Higher scores indicate greater 
satisfaction with work at the ECEC setting. 
Self-efficacy, overall 
The scale “self-efficacy, overall” (S2SELF) was constructed using staff responses (“not at all”, “to some 
extent”, “quite a bit”, “a lot”) to ten statements about the different tasks they could do in their work with 
children (SS2G24A, SS2G24B, SS2G24C, SS2G24D, SS2G24E, SS2G24F, SS2G24G, SS2G24H, 
SS2G24I and SS2G24J): “Support the development of children from disadvantaged backgrounds”; “Adapt 
your work to individual child needs”; “Help children develop their capacity to learn independently”; “Help 
children prepare for transitioning to other <ECEC settings> or to <ISCED 2011 level 1> schools”; “Stimulate 
children’s interest in cultural differences and commonalities”; “Help children to interact with each other and 
show good social behaviour (e.g. sharing, helping others)”; “Help children develop creativity and problem 
solving”; “Use <digital resources and tools> to facilitate working with children”; “Provide all children with a 
feeling of security”; and “Use a variety of monitoring and assessment strategies to follow children’s 
development, well-being and learning”. Higher values on this scale indicate greater perceived self-efficacy. 

   299 
 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
Sources of work stress: Aspects related to capacity, resources and time 
The scale of “sources of work stress: aspects related to capacity, resources and time” (S2STRESO) as 
constructed using staff responses (“not at all”, “to some extent”, “quite a bit”, “a lot”) to five statements 
about the sources of stress at their ECEC setting (SS2G47C, SS2G47D, SS2G47G, SS2G47J and 
SS2G47L): “Having too much administrative work to do (e.g. filling out forms)”; “Having extra duties due to 
absent <ECEC staff>“; “Addressing parent or guardian concerns”; “Having too many tasks to do at the 
same time”; and “Lacking resources (e.g. financial support, material resources)”. Higher scores in this 
scale indicate greater perceived stress in terms of capacity, resources and time. 
Sources of work stress: Aspects specific to children’s development, well-being and 
learning 
The “scale of sources of work stress: aspects specific to children’s development”, well-being and learning 
(S2STRESC) as constructed using staff responses (“not at all”, “to some extent”, “quite a bit”, “a lot”) to 
five statements about the sources of stress at their ECEC setting (SS2G47E, SS2G47F, SS2G47H, 
SS2G47I and SS2G47K): “Being held responsible for children’s development, well-being and learning”; 
“Managing <classroom/playgroup/group> behaviour”; “Accommodating children with special education 
needs”; “Having too many children in my <classroom/playgroup/group>“; and “Having to adapt my 
practices with children due to unexpected disruptions (e.g. natural disasters, public health 
emergencies/pandemics, humanitarian crises”. Higher scores indicate greater perceived stress in relation 
to children’s development, well-being and learning. 
Reference 
 
OECD (forthcoming), Teaching and Learning International Survey (TALIS) 2024 Technical 
Report, OECD Publishing, Paris, forthcoming.

Annex E. List of TALIS Starting Strong 2024 
contributors 
TALIS Starting Strong is a collaborative effort, bringing together expertise from participating countries and 
subnational entities that share an interest in developing a survey project to inform their policies about the 
early childhood education and care (ECEC) workforce and quality of provision. This report is the product 
of collaboration and co-operation among many stakeholders and contributors.  
In particular, the co-operation of staff and leaders in the participating ECEC settings has been crucial in 
ensuring the success of TALIS Starting Strong. Engagement with bodies representing teachers (Education 
International) and regular briefings and exchanges with the Trade Union Advisory Council (TUAC) at the 
OECD have been very important in the development and implementation of TALIS Starting Strong.  
The Informal Group on TALIS Starting Strong, a sub-group of the TALIS Governing Board, has, in the 
context of OECD objectives, driven the development of the project and its policy objectives. This includes 
the objectives of the analysis and reports produced, the conceptual framework and the development of the 
TALIS Starting Strong questionnaires. The Informal Group on TALIS Starting Strong has also overseen 
the implementation of the survey and the preparation of this report.  
Participating countries and subnational entities implemented TALIS Starting Strong at the national or 
subnational level at national project centres through, among others, national project managers (NPMs), 
national data managers (NDMs) and national sampling managers (NSMs), who were subject to rigorous 
technical and operational procedures. The NPMs played a crucial role in helping to secure the co-operation 
of ECEC settings, to oversee the national adaptation, translation and validation of the questionnaires, to 
manage the national data collection and processing and to verify the results from TALIS Starting Strong. 
The NDMs co-ordinated data processing at the national or subnational level and liaised in the cleaning of 
the data. The NSMs were responsible for implementing TALIS Starting Strong, respecting sampling 
procedures and other rigorous technical and operational procedures. In addition to the participating 
countries and subnational entities, the United States contributed to the development of the questionnaires.  
A Questionnaire Expert Group (QEG) was established to translate the policy priorities into questionnaires 
to address the policy and analytical questions that had been agreed by the Informal Group on TALIS 
Starting Strong. A Technical Advisory Group (TAG) was assembled to advise during the decision-making 
process for technical or analytical issues. A group of subject-matter experts and analysts were also critical 
in the analytical phase and drafting of the initial reports.  
The co-ordination and management of implementation at the international level was the responsibility of 
the appointed contractors, the International Association for the Evaluation of Educational Achievement 
(IEA), its consortium members, cApStAn and RAND Europe AISBL. The TALIS Starting Strong Consortium 
included staff from the IEA offices in Amsterdam and Hamburg, cApStAn and RAND Europe AISBL.  
IEA Hamburg was responsible for the overall survey planning, survey administration, the international data 
management, item analyses and scaling for the TALIS core. IEA Amsterdam was responsible for 
overseeing the verification of the translation and for the implementation of the international quality control 
programme. The sampling unit at the IEA Hamburg, developed the sampling plan, advised countries and 
subnational entities on its application, calculated the sampling weights and advised on the calculation of 

   309 
RESULTS FROM TALIS STARTING STRONG 2024 © OECD 2025 
  
sampling errors. RAND Europe AISBL managed the development of the survey instruments and 
conceptual framework. cApStAn linguistic quality control, Brussels Belgium, was responsible for organising 
and implementing the translation verification process for all administered languages. 
The OECD Secretariat had overall responsibility for managing the project, monitoring its implementation 
on a day-to-day basis and serving as the secretariat of the Informal Group on TALIS Starting Strong.

TALIS
Results from TALIS Starting Strong 2024
Strengthening Early Childhood Education and Care
Early childhood education and care (ECEC) has the potential to address a range of goals, yet delivering on the potential 
of ECEC requires a workforce that is equipped and motivated to meet the unique demands of facilitating young 
children’s development, learning and well‑being. The OECD Starting Strong Teaching and Learning International Survey 
(TALIS Starting Strong) focuses on the ECEC workforce. In 2024, ECEC staff and leaders in 17 countries and subnational 
entities shared insights into a wide range of topics, including their working conditions, professional development 
opportunities and their work with young children.
This report presents the results of the survey, looking at the activities and supports for children and their families 
through ECEC, as well as the aspects of working in ECEC that staff appreciate, their opportunities for career progression 
and sources of work‑related stress. The governance, oversight and structure of job opportunities in the sector are also 
considered. Taking a forward‑looking approach, the TALIS Starting Strong 2024 data are used to inform strategies 
for strengthening ECEC systems in the face of shifting demands on the sector and short‑term crises, better equipping 
these systems to realise the benefits of ECEC for the future.
